On the uniform dimension of subextensions in skew polynomial rings
BertrandNguefack

Abstract
. This work investigates the invariance of the non-necessarily finite uniform dimension and related concepts for subextensions in skew polynomial rings 𝕊 = R ⁡ [ X ; α , δ ] \mathbb{S}=R[\mathbf{\mathrm{X}};\mathbf{\alpha},\mathbf{\delta}] of bijective type over a well-ordered set of variables. When the coefficient ring has enough uniform left ideals, in the commuting variables case we show that classical results on this topic for polynomial rings extend to subextensions of skew Laurent polynomial rings 𝕊 = R ⁡ [ X ± 1 ; α ] \mathbb{S}=R[\mathbf{\mathrm{X}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\mathbf{\alpha}] , generated over R R by any family of (standard) terms. The situation in the non-commuting variables context is more complex; easily formed polynomial-like subrings can behave very oddly from the ambient ring. We provide easy examples of a (semi)prime left Goldie skew polynomial ring of bijective type containing a monoid subring isomorphic to a free non-commutative polynomial ring. We then study the so-called subclass of essentially special subextensions and obtain for them the preservation of the uniform dimension and related concepts.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
16P60, 16P70, 16S36, 16S38

1.
Introduction

Throughout this paper, every ring is associative and has an identity;
R
denotes a ground ring (of coefficients); a ring extension of
R
is alternatively termed as an
R
-ring
. An
R
-ring morphism is any ring
map between
R
-rings that restricts to the identity map on the ground ring; a subextension of an
R
-ring
S
is generally any
R
-subring of
S
.
The uniform (or Goldie) dimension
\mathsf{udim}(M)
of a module
M
(over some ring) is the supremum of those cardinal numbers
\mathfrak{N}
such that
M
contains an
\mathfrak{N}
-indexed direct sum of non-zero submodules
[
GW04
,
DGALP06
]
. Except otherwise specified, the uniform dimension of a ring
S
mean its left uniform dimension denoted and given by
\mathsf{udim}(S)=\mathsf{udim}({{}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}S})
.
Left Goldie conditions for a ring mean the finiteness of the uniform dimension together with the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators.
It is well-known that a subring needs not have the same uniform dimension as
the ambient ring. Starting with a classical paper by
[
Sho72
]
on polynomial rings with finite right uniform dimension, the invariance of the uniform dimension and the preservation of Goldie conditions under ring extensions has been an active research topic till date. Due to the importance of polynomial rings, and more generally, of skew polynomial rings and related polynomial-like rings,
Shock’s result was immediately extended to group rings by
[
Wil73
]
, and latter on, to (finitely) iterated Ore extensions of bijective type by
[
Mat95
]
; the case of iterated Ore extensions of injective type over semiprime left Goldie ring was dealt with by
[
LM03
]
, and that of skew PBW extensions is started by
[
Rey14
]
. It is a well-established fact that the uniform dimension and Goldie conditions have important applications in ring theory itself
[
Jat72
,
CC73
,
Sho74
,
CG86
,
MR87
,
Ker90
,
Fai96
,
VHJLP03
]
and references therein, as well as in module theory
[
Cam77
,
AHJLP92
,
LM04
]
.

Polynomial-like subrings of skew polynomial rings can behave very strangely from ordinary skew polynomial rings; however they arise naturally and in various specialized shapes in algebra and geometry.
The present work considers an ambient
R
-ring
\mathbb{S}
together with its subextensions, where
\mathbb{S}
is

❍

either a multivariate skew Laurent polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
of bijective type with commuting variables,

❍

or a multivariate skew polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\delta}]
of bijective type over an arbitrary well-ordered set
\boldsymbol{X}
of independent variables.

Thus as left
R
-module,
\mathbb{S}
is free with basis given by the set
\mathbb{T}
of standard terms: for the skew polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\delta}]
,
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
consist of all ordered product
x_{1}\dotsm x_{n}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\dotsm\leq x_{n}
in
\boldsymbol{X}
; and in the case of the skew Laurent polynomial ring in commuting variables,
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
simply consists of all commutative words on the alphabet

\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}}=\boldsymbol{X}\cup\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}
with
\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptx\in\boldsymbol{X}\}
.
We then address the following problem that arises naturally.

Problem 1.1
.

Let
A
be a polynomial-like subextension of
\mathbb{S}
; essentially one may restrict attention to the case that
A
contains
R
and is generated as left
R
-module by a subset of standard terms.
Which conditions on the shape of
A
will ensure that:
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})=\mathsf{udim}(R)
, while
A
is left Goldie provided so is
R
?

A specialization of Problem
1.1
to the commuting variables context follows.

Problem 1.2
.

Let
A
be any subextension of the skew Laurent polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, generated over
R
by a family of standard terms from
\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
. Does it hold that

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
? Is it true that
A
is ((semi)prime) left Goldie precisely when so is
R
?

The feeling about Problem
1.2
is that the answer should be positive, at least when the ambient ring is simply a polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X}]
. However even in the latter case, it appears that one may not reach the expected positive answer by relying on the classical approach
[
Sho72
]
. The issue becomes highly complex and very challenging in the non-commuting variables context of Problem
1.1
.

Content and main results of the paper

In section
2
, we review the invariance of the non-necessarily finite uniform dimension for arbitrary ring extensions. Indeed in the original paper by Shock and subsequent papers on the topic, it was tacitly or explicitly assumed that the ground ring has finite uniform dimension. For a non-zero left module
M
over some ring, we say that
M
has enough uniform submodules to mean that every non-zero submodule in
M
contains a uniform submodule. A key step towards the invariance of the uniform dimension is provided by
Proposition
2.3
:

M
has enough uniform submodules if and only if
M
contains an essential submodule which is a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}
of uniform submodules for some indexing set
I
; in this case,
\mathsf{udim}(M)
equals the cardinal number
\lvert I\rvert
.
This key characterization allows to provide a general and quick strategy guaranteeing the invariance of the uniform dimension under general ring extension (Proposition
2.4
).
Specifically, we introduce and study a concept of

nicely essential subring

A
of a given ring
S
(Definition
2.5
); it results that
A
and
S
share many properties in common including the invariance of the uniform dimension, the preservation of Goldie conditions and (semi)primeness; see Propositions
2.6
and
2.7
.

Section
3
focusses on the commuting variables setting since this case is enlightening for the more complex non-commuting setting.
Relying only on the general strategy investigated in section
2
, we solve Problem
1.2
completely in Theorems
3.3
and
3.5
.

In Section
4
,
we address in full generality Problem
1.1
and formally introduce and investigate
essentially special subextensions
of
\mathbb{S}
. We then lift to this general setting the main classical results
[
Sho72
,
Mat95
]
, while allowing infinite uniform dimension. More precisely, Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
establish the invariance of the uniform dimension and the computation of left singular ideals for essentially special subextensions
A
.
The main strategy is proving the lifting of uniform or essential left ideals from the ground ring
R
to
A
, generalizing classical techniques. The final theorem
4.17
is an application to (semi)prime left Goldie rings.

Section
5

provides a family of essentially special subextensions in the two-variate case,
highlighting the fact that familiar subextensions in
\mathbb{S}
generally fail to be skew polynomial rings in any meaningful way, while still having the same uniform dimension as
\mathbb{S}
.

Though our results are sharp for an ambient skew polynomial ring of bijective type,
an obvious perspective would be to further extend the class of ‘specialized’ subextensions of
\mathbb{S}
for which the invariance of the uniform dimension holds.

2.
Invariance of the uniform dimension under general ring extensions

We consider general ring extensions
R\subset S
and provide some strategy allowing, on one hand to quickly
compare the uniform dimension of both rings
R
and
S
, and on the other hand to derive Goldie conditions for subrings of left Goldie rings.

While
\mathbb{N}
denotes the set of natural numbers including
0
, for
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
we write
\llbracket m\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn\rrbracket
for the range of all integers
k
with
m\leq k\leq n
. For every subset
U
of in a left
S
-module
M
, the
left annihilator
of
U
in
S
is the left ideal

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(U)=\{a\in S\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptaU=0\}
; it is an ideal of
S
whenever
U
is a submodule in
M
. We usually omit the subscript in

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(U)
when
S
is the base ring or when it is implied by the context. The
singular submodule
of
M
is defined as
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(M)=\{x\in M\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\mathPzc{a}(x)\text{ contains an essential left ideal in }S\}
.
We write
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}({{}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}S})
for the left singular ideal of the ring
S
. The notation for singular submodule is being borrowed from
[
MR87
, §2.2.4]
.

Remark 2.1
.

The
ascending chain condition
and the
descending chain condition
on left annihilators are inherited by subrings.
Thus, a subring of a left Goldie ring is left Goldie if and only it has finite uniform dimension.

2.1.
Module with enough uniform submodules

Definition 2.2
.

We say that a non-zero module
M
over some ring
has enough uniform submodules
provided every non-zero submodule in
M
contains a uniform submodule.
Likewise, a ring
S
has enough uniform left ideals
if every non-zero left ideal in
S
contains a uniform left ideal of
S
.

Our first key result characterizes those modules whose uniform dimension is attained (in a strong sense), generalizing the finite dimensional case.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
M
be any non-zero left module over some ring. Then
M
has enough uniform submodules if and only if
M
contains an essential submodule which is a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}
of uniform submodules for some indexing set
I
. In this case,
\mathsf{udim}(M)
coincides with the cardinal number
\lvert I\rvert
.

Proof.

Start with the assumption that
M
has enough uniform submodules. Thus the family of uniform submodules of
M
is non-empty and, by an obvious use of Zorn’s lemma, we may consider a maximal independent set
\mathcal{U}=\{M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pti\in I\}
of uniform submodules in
M
. Then the submodule
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
must be essential in
M
.
Indeed assuming the contrary, there will exist a non-zero submodule
N\subset M
with
(\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\cap N=0
, while by assumption
N
contains some uniform submodule

N^{\prime}
. This yields a larger independent set
\mathcal{U}\cup\{N^{\prime}\}
, contradicting the maximality of
\mathcal{U}
. Hence as expected,

M
contains an essential submodule which is a direct sum of uniform submodules.

Conversely, assume that
M
contains a direct sum
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}
of uniform submodules such that
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}
is essential in
M
.
Our gold is to prove that every non-zero submodule in
M
contains a uniform submodule and
\mathsf{udim}(M)=\lvert I\rvert
.
In the case of a finite cardinal number
n\geq 1
, it is well-known
[
GW04
, Lemma 5.16, Proposition 5.20]
that: a module has uniform dimension
n
precisely when it contains an essential submodule which is a direct sum of
n
uniform submodules.
Thus we may continue with the case that
\lvert I\rvert
is infinite.
Let
N\subset M
be any non-zero submodule; for every finite subset
\Lambda\subset I
we also let
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
. Since
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}
is essential in
M
, the module
N\cap M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}=N\cap(\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is non-zero and consequently, for some finite non-empty subset
\Lambda\subset I
the module
N\cap M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}
is non-zero as well. But then (by the final cardinal case),
\mathsf{udim}(M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}})=\lvert\Lambda\rvert
is finite and
\mathsf{udim}(N\cap M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}})\leq\lvert\Lambda\rvert
is also finite and non-zero. Thus,
N\cap M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}
contains a uniform submodule.

Now assuming that
M
contains a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s\in I^{\prime}}}}M_{s}^{\prime}
of non-zero submodules with
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert\geq\lvert I\rvert
,
our next aim is to prove that
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert I\rvert
. Write
\aleph_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}0}}}=\lvert\mathbb{N}\rvert
for the countable cardinal; for each finite number
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
set
\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}=\{\Lambda\subset I\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\lvert\Lambda\rvert=n\}
.
For every
s\in I^{\prime}
, since
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}^{\prime}
is a non-zero submodule of
M
while
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}
is an essential submodule of
M
, the module
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}^{\prime}\cap M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}I}}}
must contain a non-zero cyclic submodule
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}
and there is some finite subset
\Lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s}}}}\subset I
with
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\subset M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s}}}}}}}
.
Next fix
1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
n
-element subset
\Lambda\subset I
, form the subset:

I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}=\{s\in I^{\prime}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptN_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\subset M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}\}
, thus the finite direct sum
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of uniform submodules contains the direct sum
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}}}}}=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s\in I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}}}}N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}
of non-zero cyclic submodules.
But since
M_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}
cannot contain a direct sum of more than
n
non-zero submodules, it holds that
\lvert I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}\rvert\leq n
.
Since moreover each element
s\in I^{\prime}
is by definition a member of
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s}}}}}}}^{\prime}
, we also have that

I^{\prime}=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}}}\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}}}}I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}
.
But for each
1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
, the set
I
clearly embeds into each
\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}
and the latter embeds into the
n
-fold Cartesian product
I^{n}
, and since
I
is an infinite cardinal it follows that
\lvert I\rvert\leq\lvert\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}\rvert\leq\lvert I^{n}\rvert=\lvert I\rvert
. Hence for each
1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
and every

\Lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}
, we have shown that
\lvert I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}\rvert\leq n
and
\lvert\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}\rvert=\lvert I\rvert
, whence the following:

\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}}}}\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda\in\mathcal{E}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}}}}I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\Lambda}}}^{\prime}\rvert\leq\aleph_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}0}}}\lvert I\rvert=\lvert I\rvert.

This shows that
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert=\lvert I\rvert
, completing the proof of the proposition.
∎

2.2.
General strategies ensuring the invariance of the uniform dimension and related concepts

The classical strategy successful for investigating the invariance of the finite uniform dimension for skew polynomial rings is summarized for arbitrary ring extensions by the following proposition, allowing infinite uniform dimension.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
A
be any subring of an
R
-ring such that
A\cap AL
is essential as left
A
-submodule in
AL
for every non-zero left ideal
L
in
R
, and for all left ideals
L,L^{\prime}
with

L\cap L^{\prime}=0
,
AL\cap AL^{\prime}=0
.
Then
\mathsf{udim}(R)\leq\mathsf{udim}(A)
. Assume additionally that if
L
is any uniform or essential left ideal in
R
, then so is the left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
.
Then, if
R
has enough uniform left ideals
then so does
A
and

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

Proof.

To see that
\mathsf{udim}(R)\leq\mathsf{udim}(A)
, given any direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of non-zero left ideals in
R
, we quickly check that one gets a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
of non-zero left ideals in
A
. By assumption, each
A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
is already a non-zero left ideal in
A
for all
i\in I
.
And if
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}
are pairwise distinct indices from
I
for some integer
n\geq 2
, then for every
s\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn\rrbracket
, since
L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}}}}\cap\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k\neq s}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k}}}}}}=0
, the first assumption from the proposition grants that
AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}}}}\cap\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k\neq s}}}AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k}}}}}}=0
. Thus the sum
\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is still a direct sum of non-zero left ideals in
A
.

Next continue by assuming that
R
has enough uniform left ideals and, if
L
is a uniform (or an essential) left ideal in
R
then so is the left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
.
Thus by virtue of Proposition
2.3
, the uniform dimension of
R
is attained in the stronger sense that
R
contains an essential left ideal arising as direct sum
L=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of uniform left ideals, with
\lvert I\rvert=\mathsf{udim}(R)
. But then, the left ideal
A\cap AL
is essential in
A
and it contains the direct sum
\widetilde{L}=\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
of uniform left ideals of
A
. Let us show that
\widetilde{L}
is also essential as left ideal in
A
.
Given any non-zero left ideal
J
in
A
, since
J\cap AL=J\cap A\cap AL\neq 0
, there is a finite non-empty subset
\Lambda\subset I
such that
J\cap\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\neq 0
. But since by assumption each
A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
is an essential submodule of the left
A
-module
AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for all
i\in\Lambda
, it follows that

\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is still an essential submodule of the left
A
-module
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
,
whence
J\cap\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in\Lambda}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\neq 0
as well. Thus we have shown that
\widetilde{L}
is an essential left ideal in
A
, arising as
I
-indexed direct sum of uniform left ideals. Now another invocation of Proposition
2.3
yields that

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\lvert I\rvert=\mathsf{udim}(R)
and
A
has enough uniform left ideals.
∎

It is well-known that the uniform dimension is
preserved under left localization with respect to a left Ore set of regular elements,
[
MR87
, Lemma 2.2.12]
.
A slightly more general situation is captured by the following concept of nicely essential ring extension, as we will show.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
L
be a submodule of a left
R
-module
M
. We say that
L
is
nicely essential in
M
or
M
is a
nicely essential extension
1
1

1

An alternative terminology could have been ‘
strongly essential submodule
’, but the latter already appears in few places in the literature (for different purposes and) to mean a submodule
L
of an
R
-module
M
such that every
I
-indexed direct product of copies of
L
is essential in the corresponding
I
-indexed direct product of copies of
M
for all set
I
.
For a submodule
L
of an
R
-module
M
, while the condition for
L
to be ‘strongly essential’ in
M
does not implies the property for
L
to be a nicely essential submodule in
M
, the latter is evidently stronger than requiring that every direct sum of copies of
L
is essential in the corresponding direct sum of copies of
M
.

of
L
provided for every finite set

E\subset M
of non-zero elements, there exists
a\in R
with

0\neq ax\in L
for all
x\in E
.
If additionally it holds for every
x\in M
that
cx\in L
for some left regular element
c
of
R
, then we call
L
a

strongly nicely essential
submodule of
M
, (lacking a more shorter terminology).

By a (
strongly
)
nicely essential subring
of a ring
S
we mean any subring
A
that is a (strongly) nicely essential left
A
-submodule in
S
. In this case, we also call
S
a (
strongly
)
nicely essential ring extension
of
A
.

Proposition 2.6
.

The following statements hold for a nicely essential subring
A
of a ring
S
.

\mathsf{(a)}

If
L,L^{\prime}
are left ideals in
A
with
L\cap L^{\prime}=0
then
SL\cap SL^{\prime}=0
;
\mathsf{udim}(S)=\mathsf{udim}(A)
. The left singular ideal
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
is a nicely essential left
A
-submodule in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
with
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
.

\mathsf{(b)}

Let
L\subset S
be any non-zero left
A
-submodule. Then the left ideal
A\cap L
is uniform (or resp., essential) in
A
if and only if so is the left ideal
SL
in
S
.

Proof.

Starting with the first claim, since
A
is at least essential as left
A
-submodule in

S
, it is already granted that
\mathsf{udim}(S)\leq\mathsf{udim}(A)
. Next assuming that
L,L^{\prime}
are two left ideals in
A
with
L\cap L^{\prime}=0
,
we will show that
SL\cap SL^{\prime}=0
. Let
v\in SL\cap SL^{\prime}
; then
v=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n^{\prime}}}}}v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}
for some
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}},v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}}^{\prime}\in S,\,u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\in L,u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}}^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}
.
The main assumption on
S
applied to the finite set
E=\{v,v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}},\ldots,v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}},v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}^{\prime},\ldots,v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n^{\prime}}}}^{\prime}\}
yields some
a\in A
such that for every
(i,j)\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn\rrbracket\times{\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn^{\prime}\rrbracket}
we have:

av_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}},av_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}}^{\prime}\in A,\,av=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(av_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n^{\prime}}}}}(av_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime})u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\in L\cap L^{\prime}
and

av
is non-zero whenever so is
v
.
But since
L\cap L^{\prime}=0
, we must have
av=0
and
v=0
as well. Hence,

SL\cap SL^{\prime}=0
. This also grants (just as in the first claim of Proposition
2.4
) that

\mathsf{udim}(A)\leq\mathsf{udim}(S)
; whence the equality,

\mathsf{udim}(S)=\mathsf{udim}(A)
.

Turning to the claim about left singular ideals,
the assumption that
A
is a nicely essential left
A
-submodule of
S
already grants for every left ideal
J
of
S
that
A\cap J
is still a nicely essential left
A
-submodule of
J
.
Also,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)\subset A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
since
A
is already an essential
A
-submodule in
S
. For the converse inclusion, let
x\in A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
and
0\neq y\in A
. The definition of the left singular ideal
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
yields some
b\in S
with
by\neq 0
and
by\cdot x=0
, so that by the assumption on
S
, there must also exist some
a\in A
with
0\neq ab,ab\cdot y\in A
while
(ab\cdot y)\cdot x=0
and
x\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
. Whence,
A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
.

Next for statement
\mathsf{(b)}
, assuming that
A\cap L
is a uniform left ideal in
A
, let us show that
SL
is uniform as left ideal in
S
. So let

f=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}f_{i}u_{i}
and
g=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{j=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}g_{j}v_{j}
be non-zero elements in
SL
with
0\neq m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,

f_{i},g_{j}\in S
and
u_{i},v_{j}\in L
for
1\leq i\leq m
,
1\leq j\leq n
.
Applying the main assumption to the finite subset
\{f,g,f_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}},g_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}}\,\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt1\leq i\leq m,\,1\leq j\leq n\}
yields some
v\in A
with
vf,vg,vf_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}},vg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}}\in A
(for all
(i,j)\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptm\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn\rrbracket
) and
vf,vg\neq 0
. In particular, the non-zero elements
vf
and
vg
already live in the uniform left ideal
A\cap L
of
A
and there are two elements
a,b\in A
with
0\neq avf=bvg
; whence
SL
is uniform as
left ideal of
S
. Conversely, assuming that
SL
is uniform as left ideal in
S
, we quickly check that
A\cap L
is uniform as left in
A
. Since
A
is already essential as left
A
-submodule in
S
,
A\cap L
must be non-zero. Given any non-zero elements
x,y\in A\cap L
, by assumption there exist
c,d\in S
with
0\neq cx=dy
and another application of the main assumption yields some

v\in A
with
vc,vd\in A
and
0\neq(vc)x=(vd)y
, which shows that
A\cap L
is a uniform left ideal in
A
.
Next, to complete the proof of statement
\mathsf{(b)}
, it remains to address the claim about essential left ideals. Since
A
is already essential as left
A
-submodule in
S
,
if
A\cap L
is essential as left ideal in
A
, then it is still essential as left
A
-submodule in
S
and so the left ideal
SL
is essential in
S
. Conversely, supposing that
SL
is essential as left ideal of
S
, let us check that
A\cap L
is essential as left ideal of
A
.
Given any non-zero
x\in A
, it holds for some
v\in S
that
0\neq vx=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}v_{i}u_{i}\in SL
for some positive
m\in\mathbb{N}
and some

(v_{i},u_{i})\in S\times L
with
v_{i}u_{i}\neq 0
for
1\leq i\leq m
. The main assumption on
S
applied to the subset
\{vx,v_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt1\leq i\leq m\}
yields some
a\in A
with

0\neq avx,av_{i}\in A
for all
i\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptm\rrbracket
, so that,
0\neq avx=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}(av_{i})u_{i}\in A\cap L
and
A\cap L
is essential as left ideal in
A
as expected. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We now characterize (semi)primeness under nicely essential extension. Here,
‘(semi)prime’ is a short form for ‘prime (or respectively, semiprime)’.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
A
be a nicely essential subring of a ring
S
. Then, if
A
is (semi)prime then so is
S
; if additionally
A
is left Goldie then so is
S
. Conversely, if
S
is left Goldie, then so is
A
. If
S
is (semi)prime left Goldie, then so is every strongly nicely essential subring in
S
.

Proof.

Starting with the first statement of the proposition and assuming that
A
is prime, we will show that so is
S
. Let
I,J
be ideals in
S
with
IJ=0
and
J\neq 0
. Since
A
is essential as left
A
-submodule in
S
,
J\cap A
is a non-zero ideal of
A
while
(I\cap A)(J\cap A)=0
, which yields that
I\cap A=0
and
I=0
. Hence,
S
is prime. The corresponding statement for semiprimeness follows by letting
I=J
. Now, since a prime ring is semiprime while (by Goldie’s theorem
[
MR87
, §2.3.6]
) a semiprime left Goldie ring is precisely a semiprime ring with finite uniform dimension and with zero left singular ideal, the second part of the first statement of the proposition follows thanks to
Proposition
2.6
\mathsf{(a)}

For the converse, suppose that
S
is left Goldie. Then since the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators is inherited by subrings,
A
is left Goldie by virtue of Proposition
2.6
\mathsf{(a)}
. Now assume the additional property that

A
is a strongly nicely essential subring in
S
.
Assuming that
S
is semiprime and given any ideal
N
in
A
with
N^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}=0
, let us show that
N
is the zero ideal.
The right annihilator ideal
I=\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(N)
is known to be essential as left ideal in
A
. Indeed for each non-zero left ideal
L
in
A
, it holds for some
k\in\{0,1\}
that
N^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}L\neq 0
and
N^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k+1}}}L=0
, hence

0\neq N^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}L\subset L\cap\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}(N)
.
It follows by
Proposition
2.6
\mathsf{(b)}
that

SI
is also an essential left ideal of
S
. But then, because
S
is semiprime left non-singular with finite uniform dimension, we know
(thanks to
[
MR87
, Proposition 2.3.5]
) that

SI
contains some regular element
c
. Write
c=u_{1}a_{1}+\dotsm+u_{n}a_{n}

with
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\in S
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in I
.
By the additional assumption on
A
, there are left regular elements
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
of
A
with

c_{n}u_{n},c_{n-1}c_{n}u_{n-1},\ldots,c_{1}\dotsm c_{n}u_{1}\in A
.
In particular, the product
c^{\prime}=c_{1}c_{2}\dotsm c_{n}c
is necessarily a left regular element of
A
that now lives in
I
; thus
Nc^{\prime}=0
which yields
N=0
as expected. Finally, assuming that
S
is prime, we wish to show that
A
is prime as well. We know (by Goldie’s theorem
[
MR87
, §2.3.6]
) that the set
\mathscr{C}(S)
of all regular elements of
S
is a left Ore set and the associated left Goldie quotient
Q
of
S
is a simple ring. The following arguments generalize those proving that
S
is prime from the assumption that
Q
is simple.
Call an element
c\in S
,
left
A
-regular
provided the left annihilator
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(c)=\{a\in A\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptac=0\}
vanishes. Let
I,J
be any non-zero ideals in
A
. Since
Q
is simple, we have
QJQ=Q
and

1=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}d_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for some positive
n\in\mathbb{N}
and some
u_{i},v_{i}\in S
,

d_{i},s_{i}\in\mathscr{C}(S)
and
a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\in J
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Letting
d\in\mathscr{C}(S)
be a common left denominator for the
d_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
’s, we get that
d=d\times 1=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}v_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for some
u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\in S
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Now applying the additional assumption for
A
, there are left regular elements
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
of
A
with

c_{n}u_{n}^{\prime},c_{n-1}c_{n}u_{n-1}^{\prime},\ldots,c_{1}\dotsm c_{n}u_{1}^{\prime}\in A
.
But then, since moreover
d
is a regular element of the larger ring
S
, the product
c=c_{1}c_{2}\dotsm c_{n}d
is necessarily a left
A
-regular element which now lives in
JQ
. It follows in particular that
IJQ\supset Ic\neq 0
. Hence
IJ\neq 0
and
A
is prime. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

2.3.
Easy examples illustrating the invariance of the uniform dimension or its failure

Example 2.8
.

Fix
1\leq q\in\mathbb{N}
and two
q
-roots of unity
\varepsilonup,\varepsilonup^{\prime}
in the center of
R
. Consider the skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}=R[x,y][z;\alpha]
where
\alpha
is the

R
-ring automorphism with
\alpha(x)=\varepsilonup y
and
\alpha(y)=\varepsilonup^{\prime}x
; clearly
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{2q}}}}
is the identity map.

The subextension
A\subset\mathbb{S}
generated by
x,z
is free as left
R
-module and its left
R
-basis is the following subset of terms:

\mathsf{T}=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}p}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptm,n,p\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and, if }n\neq 0\text{ then }p\neq 0\}
.

The ring
A
cannot be presented as skew polynomial ring (even if one allows an infinite set of variables).
But interestingly, the term
z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2q}}}\in\mathsf{T}
is a central element of
\mathbb{S}
with
z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2q}}}\mathbb{S}\subset A
. Thus
A
is a nicely essential subring in
\mathbb{S}
, in particular Proposition
2.6
grants that

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})
.

Example 2.9
.

A three-variate (semi)prime left Goldie graded skew polynomial ring of bijective type over
R
admitting a monoid
R
-subring that is isomorphic to a free non-commutative polynomial ring.

Modifying the previous example, consider the graded Ore extension
\mathbb{S}=R[x,y][z;\alpha]
where this time
\alpha
is the

R
-ring automorphism with
\alpha(x)=y
and
\alpha(y)=x+y
.
Thus we know that
\mathbb{S}
is (semi)prime left Goldie precisely when so is the coefficient
R
. Letting
(a_{n})_{n}
denote the Fibonacci series with
a_{0}=0,a_{1}=1
and
a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n+1}}}=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n-1}}}+a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}
for all
n\geq 1
, we have:

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(x)=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n-1}}}x+a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}y\text{ and }\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(y)=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n+1}}}}(x)=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}x+a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n+1}}}y\text{ for }1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}.

It easily follows that
\alpha
will have finite order precisely when
R
has a non-zero characteristic. Assume that
R
has characteristic
0
, and let
u=xz,\,v=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z
.

Then the terms
u
and
v
lie in the monoid
\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{S})
of all regular elements of
\mathbb{S}
since the variables
x,y,z
are regular in
\mathbb{S}
. We claim that the submonoid
\Gamma=\cramped{\langle u,v\rangle}\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{S})
generated by
u
and
v
is the free monoid on two non-commuting variables. Indeed given any
k
-length word
w=u_{1}u_{2}\dotsm u_{k}
with
u_{i}\in\{u,v\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, we have:
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}z}}}(u_{i})=1
and

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}z}}}(w)=k
; thus two words in the alphabet
\{u,v\}
, with different lengths, cannot be equal as elements of
\Gamma
.
Next, there are exactly four distinct elements of
\Gamma
having length
2
:

u^{2}=xyz^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}},\,uv=xy^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}},vu=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}yz^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}\text{ and }v^{2}=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}.

The
8
three-length words in terms of
u
and
v
are also pairwise distinct in
\Gamma
:

\displaystyle u^{3}

\displaystyle=xy(x+y)z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}},

\displaystyle v^{3}

\displaystyle=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}(x+y)^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}};

\displaystyle u^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}v

\displaystyle=xy(x+y)^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}},

\displaystyle v^{2}u

\displaystyle=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}(x+y)z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}

\displaystyle uvu

\displaystyle=xy^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}(x+y)z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}},

\displaystyle vuv

\displaystyle=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y(x+y)^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}},

\displaystyle uv^{2}

\displaystyle=xy^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}(x+y)^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}},

\displaystyle vu^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}

\displaystyle=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y(x+y)z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}3}}}.

Let
\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\alpha}}}}
be the submonoid of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{S})
generated by the elements
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(x),\,1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For every
k
-length word
w=u_{1}u_{2}\dotsm u_{k}
with
k\geq 3
and
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\in\{u,v\}
,

there exists some
\tau\in\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\alpha}}}}
with
w=x\tau z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
if
u_{1}=u
or
w=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}\tau z^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
if
u_{1}=v
.

Since moreover,
\gcd(x,\tau)=1
for all
\tau\in\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\alpha}}}}
,
these last observations together with the already computed
2
-length words in the alphabet
\{u,v\}
show that for all
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\in\{u,v\}
, the products

u_{1}\dotsm u_{k}
and
v_{1}\dotsm v_{l}
are equal in
\mathbb{S}
precisely when
k=l
and
u_{i}=v_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Hence the submonoid
\Gamma=\cramped{\langle u,v\rangle}\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{S})
is the free monoid on two non-commuting variables. Consequently, the monoid subring
R[u,v]=R\Gamma\subset\mathbb{S}
is a two-variate free non-commutative polynomial ring, and it has infinite left or right uniform dimension (even when the ambient ring is (semi)prime left Goldie).

The subring
R[u,v]
in the previous example is a very easy example of a monoid
R
-subextension of
\mathbb{S}
generated by a subset of standard terms that obviously fails to be
essentially special
in
\mathbb{S}
(as introduced by Definition
4.7
). This shows that the main results of section
4.3
are also sharp.

For (semi)prime left Goldie skew polynomial rings of injective type over
R
, it is even more easy to form monoid
R
-subextensions with infinite uniform dimension.

Example 2.10
.

A family of two-variate (semi)prime left Goldie skew polynomial rings of injective type over
R
containing a two-variate free non-commutative polynomial ring.

Fix an integer
e\geq 2
and consider the monoid ring
\mathbb{S}=R\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}
where
\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}=\cramped{\langle x,y\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptyx=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}y\rangle}
is the quotient of the free monoid on two generators
x,y
modulo the relation that
yx=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}y
. Alternatively,
\mathbb{S}=R[x,y;\alpha]=R[x][y;\alpha]
is the graded skew polynomial ring where
\alpha:R[x]\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}R[x]
is the
R
-ring injective map with
\alpha(x)=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}
.
Thus when the coefficient ring
R
is semiprime left Goldie, we know that
\mathbb{S}
is semiprime left Goldie and
\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})=\mathsf{udim}(R)

[
LM03
, Theorems 3.4, 3.8]
.
Next, we claim that the terms
xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y
freely generate a non-commutative submonoid
\cramped{\langle xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y\rangle}\subset\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}
.
Given any
k
-length word
w=u_{1}\dotsm u_{k}
with
u_{1}\ldots u_{k}\in\{xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y\}
, one observes that
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}y}}}(u_{i})=1
and

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}y}}}(w)=k
, so that, two words in the alphabet
\{xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y\}
with different lengths cannot be equal as elements of
\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}
. Next,
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(w)\equiv\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(u_{1})\mod e
(while
1=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(xy)\neq\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y)=2
).
These two observations show that for all
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
and terms
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},\,v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\in\{xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y\}
, the products

u_{1}\dotsm u_{k}
and
v_{1}\dotsm v_{l}
are equal in
\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}
precisely when
k=l
and
u_{i}=v_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
; which yields our claim.
Consequently, the monoid subring
R[xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y]\subset R\Gamma_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}
is a two-variate free non-commutative polynomial ring over
R
;
and
R[xy,x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}2}}}y]
has infinite left or right uniform dimension.

3.
Subextensions in skew Laurent polynomial rings

We fix an ambient skew Laurent polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, always assumed of bijective type; here
\boldsymbol{X}
is an arbitrary non-empty set of freely commuting variables over
R
,

\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}}=\boldsymbol{X}\cup\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}
with
\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptx\in\boldsymbol{X}\}
.

is a ring automorphism with

The skew Laurent polynomial ring
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
may be alternatively termed as the skew monoid ring extension of
R
by the free abelian
group
\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
over
\boldsymbol{X}
:

\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k_{1}}}}}\dotsm x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k_{n}}}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptn\in\mathbb{N},\,k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}},\ldots,x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}\in\boldsymbol{X}\}.

Thus, a subextension of
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
(generated over
R
by a family of terms from
\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
) is precisely a skew monoid ring extension of
R
by a submonoid
\mathsf{T}
in a free abelian group.
Every skew polynomial ring of bijective type and in commuting variables embeds into a skew Laurent polynomial ring; see Proposition
3.2
below.

For the investigation of left singular ideals and semiprimeness of subextensions in
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, it shall be necessary to consider a well-ordering ‘
\leq
’ on
\boldsymbol{X}
, which is always possible (at least by axiom of choice). Then the latter is extended to a linear ordering on

\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}}
by setting
y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}<x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}
for all
all variables
x,y\in\boldsymbol{X}
with
x<y
.
The induced reverse lexicographic ordering on
\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
is defined as it follows. For every natural number
n\geq 1
and every
n
-subset or
n
-tuple
\boldsymbol{x}

formed by variables
x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}<\dots<x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}
in
(\boldsymbol{X},\leq)
,
for all
n
-tuples
\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}),\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}=(k_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,k_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}
,
setting
\boldsymbol{x}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\boldsymbol{k}}}}=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k_{1}}}}}\dotsm x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k_{n}}}}}

we have:

\boldsymbol{x}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\boldsymbol{k}}}}<\boldsymbol{x}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}}\iff\,\exists j\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptn\rrbracket,\,k_{j}<k_{j}^{\prime}\text{ and }k_{i}=k_{i}^{\prime}\text{ for }j<i\leq n.

and the associated leading coefficient
is
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)=a_{p}
.
For convenience, we usually set:
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(0)=0=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(0)
and
0<\tau
for all
\tau\in\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
.

Remark 3.1
.

We get a linearly ordered free abelian group

(\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}}),\leq)
: the linear-ordering on
\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
is compatible with the multiplication in the sense that,

\text{for all }\lambda,\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}}),\text{ if }\tau<\tau^{\prime}\text{ then }\lambda\tau<\lambda\tau^{\prime}.

Thus, the leading term function

is a pseudo valuation on
R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
; more precisely
for all
0\neq f,g\in R[\mathsf{T},\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
,
it holds that

fg=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)}}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(g))\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)+h
for some polynomial
h
with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(h)<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)
.

By a
left normal element
in a monoid
\Gamma
we mean any
\upsilon\in\Gamma
with
\upsilon\Gamma\subset\Gamma\upsilon
.
We include the following proposition which should be well-known.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
S=R[\Gamma;\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
be a skew monoid ring of injective type.

\mathsf{(a)}

If
\Gamma
is left or right cancellative, then each
\lambda\in\Gamma
is left (or resp., right) regular as element of
S
.

\mathsf{(b)}

Assume that
\Gamma
is cancellative.
Then the subset
\mathsf{Z}
of left normal terms in
\Gamma
is a left Ore set of regular elements for
S
. If each conjugation map
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}
for
\upsilon\in\mathsf{Z}
is bijective, then the left ring of fractions
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
is a skew monoid ring
R[G;\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
with
G=\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}\Gamma=\{\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt(\upsilon,\lambda)\in\mathsf{Z}\times\Gamma\}
and
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}\lambda}}}}=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\circ\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}
for all
(\upsilon,\lambda)\in\mathsf{Z}\times\Gamma
.

Proof.

Part
\mathsf{(a)}
of the proposition easily follows by a direct verification. Turning to part
\mathsf{(b)}
, assume that
\Gamma
is cancellative. Then it is immediate by
\mathsf{(a)}
that every element of
\Gamma
is regular as element of
S=R[\Gamma;\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
. Let
\mathsf{Z}
consists of all left normal elements in
\Gamma
and observe that
\mathsf{Z}
is already closed under multiplication. Clearly, for every
\upsilon\in\mathsf{Z}
and
f\in S
, it holds for some
f^{\prime}\in S
that
\upsilon f=f^{\prime}\upsilon\in S\upsilon\cap\mathsf{Z}f
. We conclude that
\mathsf{Z}
is a left Ore set of regular elements for
S
; in particular one can form the left ring of fractions
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
. For the last claim in
\mathsf{(b)}
, we now suppose for every
\upsilon\in\mathsf{Z}
that each conjugation map
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}:R\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.50002pt}{2.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{(speciallabnode.north)}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{12.50002pt}{2.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{59}\lxSVG@closescope };
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{24.00005pt}{-1.72pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}R
is bijective.
Given each
\upsilon\in\mathsf{Z}
, for every term
\lambda\in\Gamma
there is a unique term

\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}
with
\upsilon\lambda=\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}\upsilon
; and for every scalar
a\in R
we have
\upsilon a=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}(a)\upsilon
.
In particular, it holds in
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
that:

\lambda\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}\text{ and }\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}a=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(a)\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\text{ for all }a\in R.

We obtain in
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
a submonoid
G=\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}\Gamma=\{\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt(\upsilon,\lambda)\in\mathsf{Z}\times\Gamma\}
which contains
\Gamma
as submonoid;
and
G
is a left
R
-generating set for
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
.
To complete the proof, it remains to see that
G
freely generates the left
R
-module

\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
. Thus let
f=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}=0
with

0\neq n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a\in R
and
(\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}},\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})\in\mathsf{Z}\times\Gamma
such that the
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}
’s are pairwise distinct elements of
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
. We must check that the
a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
’s are all zero. Since
\mathsf{Z}
consists of left normal elements of
\Gamma
, the product-term

\upsilon=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}\dotsm\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}
readily serves as common left denominator for the
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}
’s and we have:

\upsilon\cdot\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}=\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{\prime}
for some pairwise distinct
\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{\prime}\in\Gamma
,
1\leq i\leq n
. It follows that:

0=\upsilon f=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}^{\prime}
, showing that

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\upsilon}}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})=0
and
a_{i}=0
for all
i
.
Hence
G
is a left
R
-basis for
\mathsf{Z}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}S
and the latter arises as skew monoid ring
R[G;\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
be any subextension in a skew Laurent polynomial ring of bijective type over
R
.
Then
\mathsf{T}
is a denominator set for
A
, the associated ring of fractions is a skew Laurent polynomial ring of bijective type
\mathbb{S}=R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, and

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})=\mathsf{udim}(R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha}])
,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathsf{T}
.
If
R
has enough uniform left ideals, then so does
A
and

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

Proof.

In view of the structure theorem for free modules over principal ideal
domains
[
Hun74
, Thm IV. 6.1]
, every subgroup in a free abelian group is still a free abelian group.
The definition of
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
yields that
\mathsf{T}
is a submonoid in some free abelian group. We can therefore form a free abelian group
G
containing
\mathsf{T}
as submonoid such that
G=\mathsf{T}\mathsf{T}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}=\{\lambda\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}\,\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\lambda,\tau\in\mathsf{T}\}
, and we may let
\boldsymbol{X}
be a basis of
G
.
One may view
\boldsymbol{X}
just as a set of feely commuting variables over
R
, and write
G=\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})
. Naturally setting
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda\tau^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}}}}}=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\tau}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}
for all
\lambda,\tau\in\mathsf{T}
, one can now form the graded skew polynomial ring
S=R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
, of bijective type and with commuting variables; recall a natural two-sided
R
-basis of
S
is given by the set
\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
(of standard) terms. Likewise we get the skew Laurent polynomial ring of bijective type

\mathbb{S}=R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
.
Obviously as clarified by Proposition
3.2
,
\mathbb{S}
is a ring of
fractions for
S=R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
with respect to
\boldsymbol{X}
, as well as for the ring
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
with respect to
\mathsf{T}
(since
G=\mathpzc{T}(\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}})=\mathsf{T}\mathsf{T}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}-1}}}
).
In particular, both rings
S
and
A
are nicely essential subextensions in
\mathbb{S}
;
more precisely,

for finite subsets
Y\subset\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
and
Z\subset\mathsf{T}
, there are terms

\lambda\in\mathsf{T}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
with

\lambda Y\subset\mathsf{T}
and
\tau Z\subset\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
.

Then Proposition
2.6
grants that

{\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})=\mathsf{udim}(S)}
,
{\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})}
and

{\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=S\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})}
.

Next, we will show that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\cdot\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
and derive a similar formula for
A
. The proof is similar to the classical case of polynomial rings
in an arbitrary set of commuting variables.
For every finite subset
\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}\subset\boldsymbol{X}
, write
S_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}}}}=R[\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
for the skew polynomial subextension of
S
generated over
R
by the variables from
\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}
.
The classical result
[
Mat95
, Theorem 2.7]
already grants that:
if
L
is a uniform or an essential left ideal in
R
, then so is the left ideal

S_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}}}}L
in
S_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}}}}
.
Since
S
is clearly a union of the skew polynomial subrings

S_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}}}}
for
\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}
running over the family of all finite subsets in
\boldsymbol{X}
,
it still holds that:

if
L
is a uniform or an essential left ideal in
R
, then so is the left ideal
SL
in
S
. Thanks to Proposition
2.6
, the same holds for
\mathbb{S}=R[\boldsymbol{X}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\pm 1}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
and
A
.

It follows immediately that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
and

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathsf{T}\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
. Proceeding by contradiction and assuming that

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
is not contained in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\cdot\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
, one can form a polynomial
h\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
with
h\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\cdot\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
.
By Remark
3.1
, the free commutative monoid

(\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}),\cdot)
enjoys a linear ordering such that:
all
0\neq f,g\in S
, we have

fg=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)}}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(g))\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)+w
for some polynomial
w
with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(w)<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)
. We may therefore let
\lambda=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(h)\in\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
and
a=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(h)
.
Since
a\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
, choose
b\in R
non-zero such that

Rb\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)=0
. We claim that
Sb\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(h)=0
, which will produce a contradiction to fact that
h
lies in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
.
Let
f=a_{1}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}+\dotsm+a_{p}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}
with
1\leq p\in\mathbb{N}
,

0\neq a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\in R
and
\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}<\dotsm<\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}
in
\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
such that

fbh=0
. Then we must have

a_{p}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}}}}}(ba)=0
, that is,

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(a_{p})ba=0
, forcing that
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(a_{p})b=0
.
Thus
a_{p}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}b=0
and
(f-a_{p}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}})b=0
, hence inductively, on gets that

a_{i}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}b=0
for all
i\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptp\rrbracket
and
fb=0
. This yields our claim that

Sb\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(h)=0
and produces the needed contradiction to the fact that
h
lives in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
.
Hence,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
and one concludes that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
.
But then, for all
f=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}+\dotsm+a_{p}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
with
(a_{i},\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})\in R\times\mathsf{T}
, there exists some term
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
with

\lambda\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}\in\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
for all
i\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptp\rrbracket
. Hence having in view the fact that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})
while
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=S\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})
, we get that

\lambda f=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{p}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\lambda\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}\in S\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
, so that for all
i\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptp\rrbracket
,
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}(a_{i})\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
and
a_{i}\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
because
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}
is an automorphism of
R
. Thus the equality
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathsf{T}
holds as well.

Finally with the additional assumption that
R
has enough uniform left ideals
we will show that the same holds for
A
and
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.
Since
\mathsf{T}
is a two-sided
R
-basis for
A
, every direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of left ideals in
R
extends to a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}\mathsf{T}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of left ideals in
A
. Moreover it is already granted (as shown more above) that
if
L
is a uniform or an essential left ideal in
R
, then so is the left ideal

AL
in
A
. Hence, Proposition
2.4
grants
that
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
and

A
has enough uniform left ideals,
completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
be any subextension of a skew Laurent polynomial ring of injective type over
R
. Suppose that
R
has the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators.
Then if
R
is (semi)prime, then so is
A
.

Proof.

Thanks to Remark
3.1
,
\mathsf{T}
is endowed with a linear-ordering and the leading term function

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}:R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

is a pseudo valuation
on
R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
. More precisely,
for all
0\neq f,g\in R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
with
\lambda=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)
and
\tau=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)
, we have:

fg=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(g))\lambda\tau+w
for some
w\in R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(w)<\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(g)
.
With these facts, the proof of the proposition continues by repeating exactly the same arguments as in the univariate case by
[
LM03
, Proposition 3.6]
.
∎

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
be any subextension in a skew Laurent polynomial ring of bijective type over a (semi)prime ring
R
. Then, the coefficient ring
R
is left Goldie precisely when so is
A
, in which case,

A
is also (semi)prime and

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

Proof.

Recall that in a ring with finite uniform dimension, every non-zero left ideal always contains a uniform left ideal. By Goldie’s theorem
[
MR87
, 2.3.6]
, a semiprime left Goldie ring is a semiprime ring with finite uniform dimension and with a zero left singular ideal. Thus if
R
is a (semi)prime left Goldie ring, then Theorem
3.3
together with Proposition
3.4
show that
the ring
A=R[\mathsf{T};\boldsymbol{\alpha}]
is also (semi)prime left Goldie
and
\mathsf{udim}(R)=\mathsf{udim}(A)
.
Conversely, assume that
A
is left Goldie. Then since already every direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of left ideals in
R
yields a direct sum

\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}\mathsf{T}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of left ideals in
A
, we get

\mathsf{udim}(R)\leq\mathsf{udim}(A)
. But as subring,
R
already inherit from
A
the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators. Hence

R
is left Goldie and, consequently,
\mathsf{udim}(R)=\mathsf{udim}(A)
by virtue of
Theorem
3.3
.
∎

4.
Essentially special subextensions in skew polynomial rings

For the sequel,
\mathbb{S}
is an ambient skew polynomial ring
of bijective type.

4.1.
Skew polynomial rings and nice polynomials

There is a need to review skew polynomial rings in an arbitrary well-ordered set
of variables.
Let
\boldsymbol{X}=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pts\in\mathfrak{n}\}
be indexed by some non-necessarily finite ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
; the well-ordering on
\boldsymbol{X}
being induced by that of the ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
.
The reader is reminded that the class of all ordinals enjoys a well-ordering ‘
\leq
’ with:

\kappa<\eta
if and only if
\kappa\in\eta
for all ordinals
\kappa,\eta
.

We have the following set of
standard terms
, as well as corresponding truncated subsets for each ordinal
\kappa\leq\mathfrak{n}
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}}}}\dotsm x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptn\in\mathbb{N},\,0\leq s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}\leq\dotsm\leq s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}<\mathfrak{n}\};

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}})\text{ with }\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pts\in\kappa\}\subset\boldsymbol{X};\text{ thus }\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathfrak{n}}}}=\mathbb{T}.

The skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}=R[\boldsymbol{X};\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\delta}]
and its truncated subextensions
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
(for ordinals
\kappa\leq\mathfrak{n}
)
are inductively described as it follows.

❍

\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=R\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is the free left
R
-module with basis
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
;
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathfrak{n}}}}
.
As rings and for a finite ordinal
\kappa=\{0,1,\ldots,k\}
,
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=R[x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}0}}};\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}},\ldots,x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k}}};\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k}}}}]

is the usual iterated Ore extension of
R
, and
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}0}}}=R
.

❍

Let
\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
be an arbitrary ordinal and assume we have defined the multiplication of the truncated skew polynomial subextension
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=R\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=R[\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}};\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\delta}]
. Then
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa+1}}}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}};\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}]
is the univariate skew polynomial ring extension of
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
, where the conjugation map
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}:\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

is an
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
-derivation.

❍

We then notice for a limit ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
that
\mathbb{S}
and any subring
A\subset\mathbb{S}
arise as increasing unions of truncated subrings:

\mathbb{S}=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
and

A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
with
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=A\cap\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
.

Remark 4.1
.

\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
, the associated conjugation map and derivation map are suitably extended to maps
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}:\mathbb{S}\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
