{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}\mathbb{S}
defined over all of
\mathbb{S}
, with
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
the ring endomorphism of
\mathbb{S}
and
\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
the
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
-derivation such that:

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}(\tau)=\tau
and
\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}(\tau)=0
for
every
\tau\in\mathbb{T}(x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\eta}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\kappa\leq\eta\in\mathfrak{n})
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every standard term
\lambda=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}}}}\dotsm x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}}}}

with
s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}\leq\dotsm\leq s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}<\mathfrak{n}
, we shall equally use the alternative notation:

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\lambda}}}}=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}}}}}\circ\dotsm\circ\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}}}}}
.

Our main assumption says that:

the skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}
is of bijective type
, that is,
each conjugation map
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
for
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
is an automorphism. In particular, each set
\mathbb{T}(x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}})=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptm\in\mathbb{N}\}
is both a left and a right
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
-basis for
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa+1}}}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
A
be any subring in
\mathbb{S}
. Then every direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of left ideals in
R
yields a direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})

of left ideals in
A
.

Proof.

We first prove the proposition when
A=\mathbb{S}
. Clearly proceeding by (transfinite) induction on the indexing ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
, the base case is empty while the limit ordinal case is immediate (since in this case
\mathbb{S}
arises as increasing union of truncated skew polynomial rings). Now turn to the case that
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1
is a successor ordinal and let
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
be a direct sum of left ideals in
R
. The induction hypothesis grants that the sum
\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
is direct while

\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}=\mathbb{S}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for all
i\in I
.
But then, the set of univariate terms
\mathbb{T}(x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}})=\{x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptn\in\mathbb{N}\}
is also a right
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
-basis of the univariate skew polynomial ring

\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}};\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}]
and it follows that the sum
\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}\mathbb{S}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
is still a direct sum of left ideals in
\mathbb{S}
. Hence the proposition is proved for
A=\mathbb{S}
. For a general subring
A\subset\mathbb{S}
, since each
A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
is a left
A
-submodule in
\mathbb{S}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for all
i\in I
, the sum
\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is direct as well.
∎

We shall consider on standard terms the
reverse lexicographic ordering

‘
\mathrel{\leq_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{revlex}}}}}}
’ induced by the well-ordering on
\boldsymbol{X}
and inductively defined as it follows. For all
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
and
x=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\in\boldsymbol{X},\lambda,\tau\in\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
and
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\lambda x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}\mathrel{<_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{revlex}}}}}}\tau x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}l}}}\iff k<l\text{ or }k=l\text{ and }\lambda\mathrel{<_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{revlex}}}}}}\tau.

We continue to denote this ordering simply by ‘
\leq
’.
As confirmed by the next technical lemma, we get in this way
a well-ordering on
\mathbb{T}
. Hence there is an induced
leading term function

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}:\mathbb{S}\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}\mathbb{T}\cup\{0\}
: for each non-zero polynomial
f=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}+\dotsm+a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}
with
0\neq m\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\neq a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}},\ldots,a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\in R
and terms
\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}>\dotsm>\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}
in
\mathbb{T}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)=\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}
; the leading coefficient of
f
is

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}
. For convenience, one sets
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(0)=0=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(0)
and
0<\lambda
for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The set
\mathbb{T}
of standard terms is well-ordered by the reverse lexicographic ordering induced by the well-ordering on
\boldsymbol{X}
.

Proof.

Assume the non-trivial case, that is,
\boldsymbol{X}
is non-empty. We reason by (transfinite) induction on well-ordered sets (or if one prefers, on ordinals). The desired result is obvious if
\boldsymbol{X}
has only one variable, (that is, if
\mathfrak{n}
is the ordinal ‘
1
’).
Suppose
\boldsymbol{X}
is not a singleton and the result holds for all proper well-ordered subset in
\boldsymbol{X}
. Let
\Sigma
be a non empty subset of
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
, whose set of ‘leading variables’ is
\mathsf{Lvar}(\Sigma)=\{x\in\boldsymbol{X}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\Sigma\cap\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\leq x}}})x\neq\varnothing\}
, where for each ordinal
s\in\mathfrak{n}
we have the truncated subsets

\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}<x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}}}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}=\{z\in\boldsymbol{X}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptz<x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\}
and

\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\leq x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}}}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s+1}}}=\{z\in\boldsymbol{X}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptz\leq x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}s}}}\}
.
By the well-ordering principle of
\boldsymbol{X}
and of
\mathbb{N}
, we may let
y
be the smallest variable in
\mathsf{Lvar}(\Sigma)
and
m
be the smallest positive integer with
\Sigma\cap\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}<y}}})y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\neq\varnothing
.
Then, form the non-empty subset
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}<y}}})\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\lambda y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in\Sigma\}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains a smallest term, say,
\lambda
.
The inductive definition of the reverse lexicographic ordering on
\mathbb{T}
readily shows that the term
\lambda y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
is the smallest term in
\Sigma
.
∎

In the process of achieving the main gold of this section,
the most difficult and important step occurs when the indexing ordinal is a successor ordinal.
So from now henceforth, we shall let
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]

be a univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type over an
R
-ring
S
,
where
S
is meant to be a specialized subextension of a multivariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type. As a basic assumption,

S
is required to be free as left
R
-module with basis denoted as
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}
.

Thus, the set
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}\mathbb{T}(x)=\{\lambda x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\lambda\in\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a left
R
-basis for
\mathscr{S}
. We regard elements of
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}\mathbb{T}(x)
as ‘
terms
’ and view
\mathscr{S}
as a ‘polynomial-like’ ring.

We now continue with a review of some key strategies useful for investigating the uniform dimension of polynomial-like rings.
For our purpose,
it shall be necessary to suitably review the concept of ‘nice polynomials’ and gather some crucial technical facts about them.

Definition 4.4
.

By an
R
-nice element
of
S
is meant any non-zero
f\in S
whose coefficients in
R
share a common left
R
-annihilator. Thus
f=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
with
0\neq a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\in R
,

\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}\in\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}
and the
\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}
’s pairwise distinct, and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}j}}})
for all
i,j\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptm\rrbracket
.
We also say that the set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{coef}}(f)=\{a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}},\ldots,a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\}
is
R
-nice
.

We start with the following first key lemma which for the purpose of the present framework includes and amplifies
[
Sho72
,
Lemma 2.1 and Proposition 2.2 ]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
L
be any essential left ideal of
R
and
E\subset R
any finite subset with
E\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\}
non-empty.
Then for a suitable
c\in R
,
cE\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\}
is a non-empty
R
-nice subset of
L
. If moreover
E
contains some element
a
with
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(E)
,
then
c\in R
may be chosen such that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(cE)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(ca)
.

Proof.

For the main part of the lemma and starting with the case that
E
contains some element
a
with
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(E)=\cap_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x\in E}}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(x)
, we will show that there is some
c\in R
such that
cE\neq\{0\}
,
cE\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\}
is
R
-nice and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(cE)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(ca)
.
Proceeding by induction on the number of non-zero elements in
E
,
since the base case is obvious,
we may further suppose that there exists some
0\neq b\in E
with
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(b)\neq\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)
. Thus pick some
c_{1}\in\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(b)\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)
; then

c_{1}E
has fewer non-zero elements than
E
,
0\neq c_{1}a\in c_{1}E
and

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(c_{1}a)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(c_{1}E)
. Hence by the way of induction,
there is some
c_{2}\in R
such that
c_{2}\cdot(c_{1}a)\neq 0
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(c_{2}c_{1}a)
is the left annihilator of every non-zero element of
c_{2}c_{1}E
, and we are done.
Next in the case that
E
does not contain any element whose left annihilator in
R
is contained in the left annihilator of
E
in
R
, one simply repeats the same arguments as before (but without any reference to
a
) to show for a suitable
c\in R

that
cE\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\}
is a non-empty
R
-nice subset.

To complete the proof of the main part of the lemma, letting
E=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}\subset R\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\}
be
R
-nice, we have to check that there is some
c\in R
with
0\notin cE\subset L
(since
cE
is still obviously
R
-nice).
Since by assumption
L
is an essential left ideal in
R
while the
a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
’s share a common left annihilator in
R
, inductively there are scalars
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
with

0\neq c_{1}a_{1}\in L,0\neq c_{2}c_{1}a_{2}\in L,\,\ldots,0\neq(c_{n}\dotsm c_{1})a_{n}\in L
. Thus letting

c=c_{n}\dotsm c_{1}
yields the desired result.
∎

We include the following characterization of left annihilators for
R
-nice multivariate polynomials.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
0\neq g\in\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
and
p=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(g)
; suppose that
p
is
R
-nice and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
, which is implied whenever
g
is
R
-nice. Then

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g)=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
provided
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(p)=S\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
.
In particular it holds for every
R
-nice polynomial
g\in\mathbb{S}
that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(g)=\mathbb{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
.

Proof.

The first claim of the proposition is proved by imitating the same arguments for the univariate analogue
[
Mat95
, Lemma 1.1]
. The proof of the second claim proceeds by (transfinite) induction on the indexing ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
, which essentially reduces to the case of a successor ordinal. For clarity let us provide the needed evidence. Let
g\in\mathbb{S}
be
R
-nice. The base of induction is trivial.
If
\mathfrak{n}
is a limit ordinal while the result holds for all ordinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
, then
\mathbb{S}
is an increasing union of truncated skew polynomial subrings
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
and the induction hypothesis applies yields that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(g)=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa<\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}}}}(g)=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa<\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)=\mathbb{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
.
Now the case that
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
is a successor ordinal has been handled by the first claim, completing the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.2.
Essentially special subextensions and nice polynomials with prescribed degrees

It’s worth recalling from
[
Mat95
]
that the natural requirement ensuring the invariance of the uniform dimension for a univariate skew polynomial ring
B=R[t;\sigma,\vartheta]
of bijective type amounts to the condition that each
Ba
contains an
R
-nice polynomial of degree one for every non-zero scalar
a\in R
. Let us examine this requirement for
R
-subextensions
A\subset\mathscr{S}=S[x,\alpha,\delta]
. By assumption
\alpha
is bijective; but only the injectivity of
\alpha
will be enough here. We call
n\in\mathbb{N}
an
attainable
x
-degree in
A
if there exists
f\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)=n
. We set:

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)

\displaystyle=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\text{$n$ is an attainable $x$-degree in $A$}\}\text{ and, }

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}

\displaystyle=\gcd(\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\})\text{ if }A\not\subset S;\text{ otherwise, }\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(x)=0.

Clearly,
0\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)\subset\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}\mathbb{N}
.
If it happens that
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}
is not already an attainable
x
-degree in
A
, then
there will be some
0\neq n,e\in\mathbb{N}
with
n,n+e\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
but
e\notin\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
, and given an
R
-nice polynomial
f\in A
with
n=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
,
Af
cannot contain any polynomial with
x
-degree
n+e
.
Such a weird situation will definitely prevent one from adapting to
A
classical strategies necessary for studying the uniform dimension of skew polynomial rings. To resolve the last issue, we embed
A
into another subextension
\widetilde{A}\subset\mathscr{S}
where the jumps in attainable
x
-degrees disappear while
A
and
\widetilde{A}
are still close enough to guarantee the invariance of the uniform dimension.

So we arrive at the following class of specialized subextensions in skew polynomial rings, where we recall for any ring
A
that

\mathscr{C}(A)=\{u\in A:\text{ $u$ is a regular element}\}.

Definition 4.7
.

\mathsf{(a)}

By a
special subextension
of the univariate skew polynomial ring
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
we mean any
R
-subring

A
of
\mathscr{S}
with the following special shape:

A=\sum\limits_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m\in\mathbb{N}}}}\hskip-3.99994pt(A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}})
,
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)=\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}\mathbb{N}
; to each
0\neq m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
are associated some
\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A
with

\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in\mathscr{C}(S)
, some
R
-subring

A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset A\cap S
and automorphism

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
of
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
(induced by
\alpha
) such that:
for all
p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
and

q\in S
with
qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A
, it holds that

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}\bigl(\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}p-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(p)\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\bigr)<m
and
uqx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}
for some
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)
.

Notice that for
m=0
, the above requirements trivially hold with

A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(0)}}}=A\cap S
,
\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}}=1=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}0}}}
and
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(0)}}}}
given by the identity map.

\mathsf{(b)}

A (strongly) nicely essential subring of a special subextension of
\mathscr{S}
is briefly referred to as an
essentially special subextension
(or a
strongly essentially special subextension
) in
\mathscr{S}
.

\mathsf{(c)}

An (or a
strongly
)
essentially special subextension
of the multivariate skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}
is inductively defined as it follows.

✧

If the indexing ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
is the zero ordinal, then
A
is a (strongly) nicely essential subring of
R
. For a limit ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
,
A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\Lambda}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
arises as increasing union of rings with

\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{n}
such that each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa\in\Lambda
is an (or a strongly) essentially special subextension of
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
.

✧

If
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
is a successor ordinal, then
A
is a (strongly) nicely essential subring of some prescribed special subextension
\widetilde{A}
of
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}};\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}},\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}]
regarded as a univariate skew polynomial ring over
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
, while for all
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{x}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}}}}(\widetilde{A})
the associated
R
-subring
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
arises again as an (or a strongly) essentially special subextension of
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Keeping notations as in part
\mathsf{(a)}
of the above definition and letting
A
be a special subextension in
\mathscr{S}
, for each polynomial
f\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)=m
, the special shape of
A
ensures for some regular element
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
that

uf=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}p+f^{\prime}
with
0\neq p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
,
f^{\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime})<m
.

Example 4.9
.

The following observations are in order.

\mathsf{(i)}

For a univariate skew polynomial ring
R[x;\sigma,\vartheta]
of bijective type,
every
R
-subring
A
that is generated as left
R
-module
by some subset
\mathbb{T}(x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}d}}})\subset\mathbb{T}(x)
, with
d\in\mathbb{N}
, is evidently a special subextension of
R[x;\sigma,\vartheta]
.

\mathsf{(ii)}

The ambient skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}
is obviously a special subextension of itself. If
A
is any (strongly) essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
, then so is every (strongly) nicely essential subring of
A
.

\mathsf{(iii)}

Easy but non-trivial examples
are obtained as it follows: let
A\subset\mathbb{S}
, free as left
R
-module with basis
\mathsf{T}\subset\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
, have the following simple structure:
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa+1}}}=A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cdot(\mathsf{T}\cap\mathbb{T}(x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}))
for all
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
.
Then
A
is obviously a special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
, yet this ring needs not be a skew polynomial ring in the usual sense.

More involved examples of special subextensions are the object of
section
5
.

We then include the following second key lemma, a more stronger and generalized version of the ordinary univariate analogue
[
Mat95
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For the skew polynomial ring
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
of bijective type,
assume that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(p)=S\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
for each
R
-nice
p\in S
. Let
A
be any special subextension of
\mathscr{S}
, not necessarily requiring that
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}
be a member of
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
.
Then statements
\mathsf{(a)}
and
\mathsf{(b)}
below are equivalent.

\mathsf{(a)}

Aa
contains an
R
-nice polynomial of degree
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}
for all

0\neq a\in R
. In this case,
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)=\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}\mathbb{N}
.

\mathsf{(b)}

For each
R
-nice polynomial

f\in A
and all
n\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
with

n\geq\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
,
Af
contains an
R
-nice polynomial

gf
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(gf)\hskip-1.49994pt=\hskip-1.49994ptn\hskip-1.49994pt=\hskip-1.49994pt\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g)\hskip-1.49994pt+\hskip-1.49994pt\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
.

\mathsf{(c)}

Assume that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
and
\delta(\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S))=0
. Then statement
\mathsf{(a)}
holds
whenever
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}
is an attainable
x
-degree in
A
and

R\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}({\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}})}}})\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
.

Proof.

Consider the non-trivial case that the set
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
of attainable
x
-degrees in
A
does not reduce to
\{0\}
; so the natural number

\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}=\gcd(\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\{0\})
is non-zero
and
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)\subset\mathsf{d}\mathbb{N}
. For each
0\neq m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
, the special shape of
A
(Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(a)}
) grants a special element

\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A\cap\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})

with
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in\mathscr{C}(S)
, together with an induced automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
of the associated
R
-subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
such that

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}p-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(p)\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}})<m
for all

p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
.

We quickly see that
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
is a submonoid of the additive monoid
(\mathbb{N},+)
. Indeed for
0\neq m,n\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
, we have:

\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}(\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}})x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m+n}}}+\Delta
with
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}(\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}})\neq 0
,
\Delta\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta)<m+n
, showing that
m+n\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
.

Starting with the assumption that
\mathsf{(b)}
holds, let us prove
\mathsf{(a)}
. If
\mathsf{d}
is an attainable
x
-degree in
A
, then
\mathsf{(a)}
becomes a special case of
\mathsf{(b)}
. So we only need to show that
\mathsf{d}
is an attainable
x
-degree in
A
, which will also yield the equality
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)=\mathsf{d}\mathbb{N}
. Let
0\neq m\in\mathbb{N}
be smallest such that
\mathsf{d}m
is an attainable
x
-degree in
A
.
Thanks to Lemma
4.5
, we may choose an
R
-nice polynomial

w\in R\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}m}}}}\subset A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(w)=\mathsf{d}m
. Our aim is to show that
m=1
. Assuming the contrary, by the definition of
\mathsf{d}
there must exist some
\mathsf{d}n\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
with
n=ms+r
for
some
1\leq s,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
r<m
. But by assumption, we can form an
R
-nice polynomial
g=px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}ms}}}+g^{\prime}\in Aw
with
0\neq p\in S
,
g^{\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g^{\prime})<\mathsf{d}ms
; another use of the assumption yields that we can choose a polynomial
h\in A
with minimal degree, says
\mathsf{d}k
, such that
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(hg)=\mathsf{d}n=\mathsf{d}ms+\mathsf{d}r
. Remark
4.8
yields some
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d}k)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})

such that
uh=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}q+h^{\prime}
with
0\neq q\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d}k)}}}
,
h^{\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime})<\mathsf{d}k
; in particular we still have
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uh)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h)
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uhg)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(hg)=\mathsf{d}n
. But then,

\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}k}}}\in A
with

\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}\in\mathscr{C}(S)
and

uhg=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}(qp)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}\cdot(ms+k)}}}+\Delta
with
\Delta\in\mathscr{S}
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta)<\mathsf{d}\cdot(ms+k)
.
Since
g
is
R
-nice, so is its leading polynomial-coefficient
p=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(g)
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
;
the assumption about left annihilators in
S
yields that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(p)=S\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(g)
. Hence
qp
cannot be the zero polynomial because otherwise,
qg
will be
0
and
uhg=h^{\prime}g
while
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime}g)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uhg)=\mathsf{d}n
and the polynomial
h^{\prime}\in A
satisfies
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime})<\deg(h)
, contradicting the minimality of
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h)
.
Since moreover
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}
is a regular element in
S
, it follows
that
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}k}}}}(qp)\neq 0
as well, so that,

\mathsf{d}\cdot(ms+k)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uhg)=\mathsf{d}n=\mathsf{d}ms+\mathsf{d}r
, showing that
k=r
. Thus the integer
\mathsf{d}r=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h)
lies in
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
with
1\leq r<m
, a contradiction to the minimality of
m
. Hence,
m=1
and

\mathsf{d}\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
as expected.

Next assuming
\mathsf{(a)}
, we will show that
\mathsf{(b)}
holds. Since the assumption already grants that
\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)=\mathsf{d}\mathbb{N}
, given any
R
-nice polynomial
f\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)=m\in\mathbb{N}
,
it suffices to show that
Af
contains an
R
-nice polynomial

gf
with
g\in A
,
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(gf)=m+\mathsf{d}
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g)=\mathsf{d}
. Letting
p=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(f)\in S
, the polynomial
f^{\prime}=f-px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in\mathscr{S}
satisfies
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime})<m
. Since
f
is
R
-nice,
so is
p
and, picking some coefficient
a\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{coef}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
,
it holds that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(f)\text{ and }\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(f)=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(a),

where the two rightmost equalities are granted by Proposition
4.6
.
In particular, we obtain an isomorphism of left
\mathscr{S}
-modules

\mathscr{S}a\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to6.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}\mathscr{S}f
mapping each
ha
to
hf
for all
h\in\mathscr{S}
, and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(hf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(ha)
for all
h\in\mathscr{S}
. Now by
\mathsf{(a)}
, the family
\mathcal{E}=\{h\in A\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(ha)=\mathsf{d}\text{ and $ha$ is $R$-nice}\}

is non-empty.

Picking some
h\in\mathcal{E}
with minimal
x
-degree
e
, we will show that
e=\mathsf{d}
.
One may write

ha=qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}+w
with
q\in S
,
w\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(w)<\mathsf{d}
; since
ha
is
R
-nice, so is its leading polynomial-coefficient
q=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(ha)
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(ha)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(q)
, so that,

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(ha)=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(q)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q)
(still by Proposition
4.6
).
Remark
4.8
yields some
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(e)}}}
, regular in
S
and such that

uh=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}q^{\prime}+h^{\prime}
with
0\neq q^{\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(e)}}}
,

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uh)=e=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}})
,
h^{\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime})<e
.
In particular,
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uha)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(ha)=\mathsf{d}
while
uha=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}q^{\prime}a+h^{\prime}a
. We claim that
q^{\prime}a\neq 0
. If this is not the case, then since the polynomial
h^{\prime}a=uha=uqx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}+uw
has
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}
with
\deg(uw)<\mathsf{d}
while
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(uq)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(uha)
, the first key Lemma
4.5
shows for some suitable
c\in R
that
ch^{\prime}a
is
R
-nice and still has
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}
; but then, this will produce a contradiction to the minimality of
e=\deg(h)
because the polynomial
ch^{\prime}\in A
has
x
-degree less than
e
.
Hence,
q^{\prime}a\neq 0
as claimed. Now,
\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}
with
\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}\in S
regular, thus
uha=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}q^{\prime}a+h^{\prime}a=\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}(q^{\prime}a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}+h^{\prime\prime}

with
0\neq\upsilon_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{e}}}}(q^{\prime}a)\in S
,

h^{\prime\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime\prime})<e
. So,

e=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uha)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(ha)=\mathsf{d}
.

Then we may let
h=vx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}+v^{\prime}
for some
(v,v^{\prime})\in S\times A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(v^{\prime})<\mathsf{d}
; thus,

ha=v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}+h^{\prime}
with

0\neq v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)\in S
,

h^{\prime}\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h^{\prime})<\mathsf{d}
.
Since
ha
is
R
-nice, so is
v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(ha)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a))
.
It follows that
hf
is a non-zero polynomial with
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(hf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(ha)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a))
.
But we have:

hf=v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m+\mathsf{d}}}}+f^{\prime\prime}\text{ with }f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{S}\text{ and }\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime\prime})<m+\mathsf{d}.

Next, we wish to prove that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p))\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(hf)
. Arbitrary let
b\in R
with
bv\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p)=0
.
Then,
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-\mathsf{d}}}}}(bv)p=0
while for the coefficient
a\in\boldsymbol{\mathrm{coef}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
it is already granted that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p)=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)
.
It follows that
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-\mathsf{d}}}}}(bv)a=0
and

b\in\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a))=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(hf)
. Hence
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p))\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(hf)
as expected, and since moreover
hf\neq 0
we must have
v\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p)\neq 0
, so that,
hf
has
x
-degree
m+\mathsf{d}
.
Now, invoking the first key Lemma
4.5
yields a suitable scalar
c\in R
such that
chf
is
R
-nice while

cv\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(p)
is still non-zero. Thus
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(chf)=m+\mathsf{d}
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(ch)=\mathsf{d}
, completing the proof that
\mathsf{(a)}
implies
\mathsf{(b)}
.

Continuing with part
\mathsf{(c)}
of the lemma, suppose that:

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
,
\delta(\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R))=0
,
\mathsf{d}
is an
attainable
x
-degree in
A

while
R\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}({\mathsf{d}})}}})\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
.
We have to prove for every non-zero scalar
a\in R
that
Aa
contains an
R
-nice polynomial with
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}
. Setting
B=A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d})}}}
,
we are granted with some special elements
\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}\in\mathscr{C}(S)
and
\lambdaup=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}\in A\cap\mathscr{C}(\mathbb{S})
, together with an induced automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}
of the associated
R
-subring
B\subset S
such that
\lambdaup a=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\lambdaup+\Delta
with
0\neq\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\in B
,
\Delta\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta)<\mathsf{d}
. First consider the case that
a\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
. Then since by assumption
R\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(B)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
it follows that
a\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(B)
and

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(B)
.
Hence one may let
0\neq w\in B

with
Bw\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}B}}}(\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a))=0
. In particular, since
\lambdaup
is regular as element of
\mathscr{S}
, we have:

w\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\lambdaup\neq 0
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(w\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\lambdaup)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(w\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a))\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(w)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(w\lambdaup a)
.

Invoking Lemma
4.5
yields some suitable
c\in R
such that
cw\lambdaup a
is
R
-nice with
cw\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(\mathsf{d})}}}}(a)\lambdaup\neq 0
; hence

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(cw\lambdaup a)=\mathsf{d}
. We then turn to the case that
a
lies in

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the Ore-rule grants that:

x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}a=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}+\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}}}}}(a)
with

\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}}}}}(a)=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k-1}}}\delta(\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n-k}}}}(a))x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n-k}}}
.

But by assumption,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
while
the derivation map
\delta
vanishes on
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
and each automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k}}}}:S\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
