\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}S
for
k\in\mathbb{N}

sends
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
to
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)
. Consequently,
\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}}}}}(a)=0
and
x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}a=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In particular,
\lambdaup a=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}a=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}
with
0\neq\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)\in S
(because
\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}
is a regular element of
S
and
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)\neq 0
). Thus we only need to invoke Lemma
4.5
to choose a suitable
c\in R
such that
the polynomial
c\lambdaup a=c\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{\mathsf{d}}}}}(a)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\mathsf{d}}}}
is
R
-nice with degree
\mathsf{d}
. This completes the proof of
\mathsf{(c)}
, as well as the proof of the lemma.
∎

The next technical lemma is one of the most trickiest result, specific to the general setting of subextensions in skew polynomial rings, dealing with left ideals in special subextensions that are induced from the coefficient ring. This result complements the ‘nice polynomial’ strategy provided by the above lemma, and all together they are crucial for implementing the proof of the main theorems more below. Continue to keep the notations introduced by Definition
4.7
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
A
be a special subextension of
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
,

L
be any left ideal in
R
. Then
it holds for every
0\neq f\in AL
with
m=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
that

f\in\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k=0}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}(A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}})L
and there exists some
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)
such that

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(uf)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}L)\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}
, with
u=1
if
m=0
.

Proof.

Definition
4.7
already includes the requirement that
A=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k\in\mathbb{N}}}}A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
.
Let
0\neq f\in AL
, while
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
is not set. We consider the set
\mathcal{E}
of all pairs
(m,u)
defined as it follows:

❍

m\in\mathbb{N}
; if
f
is a constant polynomial (with respect to variable
x
) then
u=1
; otherwise,
u
is any element in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
that is regular as element of
S
;

❍

the polynomial
uf
expresses as sum of elements of the form
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}a
with
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}\in A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
,
a\in L
and

k\leq m
.

Since
0\neq f\in AL
, the set
\mathcal{E}
is clearly non-empty. We therefore choose a pair
(m,u)\in\mathcal{E}
with
m
minimal; in the course of the proof we will show that
m=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
. The minimality of
m
already yields that there is a non-zero polynomial
h=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}p_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
for some positive
n\in\mathbb{N}

with
p_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\neq 0
,
p_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\in S,p_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A,a_{i}\in L
for all
i
, and such that the polynomial
uf-h
is either zero (if
m=0
) or is a
sum of elements of the form
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}a
with
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}\in A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
,
a\in L
and

k<m
. Notice since
u
is a regular element of
S
that
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(uf)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
.

If
m=0
, then
uf=h\in(A\cap S)L
, in particular
f
was already constant and by the definition of
\mathcal{E}
we already have
u=1
; thus
f
lies in
(A\cap S)L
as expected. So, we continue with the case that
m\geq 1
.
In view of the special shape of
A
(Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(a)}
), the special element
\lambda=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
is regular as element of
\mathscr{S}
and we are granted with some element
u^{\prime}=u_{n}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}2}}}\dotsm u_{1}
with each
u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
lying in the associated
R
-subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset A\cap S
and regular as an element of
S
, such that

u^{\prime}p_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\lambda
for some
u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
,

1\leq i\leq n
.

Readily,
(m,u^{\prime}u)\in\mathcal{E}
while the polynomial
u^{\prime}uf-u^{\prime}h=u^{\prime}\cdot(uf-h)
expresses as
sum of elements of the form
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}a
with
px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}\in A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
,
a\in L
and
k\leq m-1
. Still by the special shape of
A
,
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
comes endowed with an induced automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
such that:

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambda p-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(p)\lambda)\leq m-1\text{ for all }p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}.

With this observation,
it holds that
u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\lambda=\lambda\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime})+\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}
for some
\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})\leq m-1
for
1\leq i\leq n
, so that,

\displaystyle u^{\prime}h

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}\lambda\cdot(\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime})+\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}=\lambda\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(v)+\Delta\text{ with }

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\text{ and }\Delta=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}\in AL,\text{ with }\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}}\in A,\,\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})\leq m-1.

Thus the minimal property of the pair
(m,u)\in\mathcal{E}
shows that the polynomial
v
just defined above must be non-zero.
But then, we also get that

u^{\prime}h=v\lambda+\Delta^{\prime}
for some
\Delta^{\prime}\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta^{\prime})\leq m-1
,
and the polynomial
u^{\prime}uf\in AL
now satisfies:

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(u^{\prime}uf)=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(v\lambda)=m
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)=m
because the element
u^{\prime}u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset A\cap S
is regular in
S
. It also follows in particular that
u^{\prime}uf
lives in
\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k=0}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}(A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}})L
as expected. Moreover,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{S}}}}(u^{\prime}uf)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=v\lambda=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}^{\prime}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})\lambda
lives in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(L)\lambda=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}L)\lambda
as claimed by the last part of the lemma.
∎

4.3.
The uniform dimension of essentially special subextensions

It helps recalling as set before that
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
is a univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type over an
R
-ring
S
that is free as left
R
-module with left
R
-basis denoted as
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}
. For our main theorems, a specific choice for
S
will be an essentially special
R
-subextension in a multivariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type. So, from now henceforth we require the additional condition:

\displaystyle\text{every $R$-nice element $p\in S$ has an $R$-nice annhilator in $S$: $\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(p)=S\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)$};

\displaystyle\text{and for all ideals
$L,L^{\prime}$ in $R$, if $L\cap L^{\prime}=0$ then
$SL\cap SL^{\prime}=0$,}

which, in view of Proposition
4.6
and
4.2
, holds for
\mathbb{S}
, as well as for all essentially special subextensions in
\mathbb{S}
as we shall prove more latter below.

We are now in the position to state the first main theorem of this section;
where for an essentially special subextension
A

of
\mathscr{S}
, we write as usual
\widetilde{A}
for a prescribed special subextension of
\mathscr{S}
containing
A
as nicely essential subring.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
A
be an essentially special subextension of
\mathscr{S}
such that
\widetilde{A}a
contains an
R
-nice polynomial of
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}}
for all
0\neq a\in R
.
Then, every uniform or essential left ideal
L
of
R
lifts to a
uniform (or resp., an essential) left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
provided the same holds for
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
for all
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\widetilde{A})
. When this is the case, if
R
has enough uniform left ideals, then so does
A
and
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

Proof.

By virtue of Proposition
2.6
it holds that:
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\widetilde{A})
, and for every left ideal
L
in
R
, the left ideal
A\cap AL
is uniform (or essential) in
A
if and only if so is the left ideal
\widetilde{A}L
in
\widetilde{A}
. Hence we only need to prove the theorem when
A=\widetilde{A}
is a special subextension in
\mathscr{S}
.
Assuming the latter, for the rest of the proof we let
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
.
The now assumed special shape of
A
grants
some special element
\lambda=\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
regular in
\mathscr{S}
, an induced automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
of the associated
R
-subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset A\cap S
, such that:

(
\star
)

\displaystyle\text{for all }p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}},\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambda p-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(p)\lambda)<m;\text{and for all }q\in S\text{ with }

\displaystyle qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A,\text{it holds for some }u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\text{ regular in $S$ that }uqx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\lambda.

Of course (in Definition
4.7
), we set:
\lambda_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}}=1=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}0}}},A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(0)}}}=A\cap S
and
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(0)}}}}=\mathsf{id}
. Also remember that the special shape of
A
already includes the requirement that

A=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k\in\mathbb{N}}}}A\cap Sx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}
.

Let
L
be any uniform left ideal of
R
that lifts to a uniform left ideal in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
for all
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
. Proving that
AL
is still uniform as left ideal in
A
.
Given non-zero polynomials
f,g\in AL
, set

k=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
,
m=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g)
. We may further suppose that
k\leq m
and, by multiplying
f
and
g
by suitable scalars from the ground ring
R
we may assume thanks to the first key Lemma
4.5
that
f
and
g
are
R
-nice.
We will proceed by ordinary induction on the natural number
k+m
to show that
Af\cap Ag\neq 0
.

If
k+m=0
then
f,g\in AL\cap S
while the technical Lemma
4.11
shows that
AL\cap S=(A\cap S)L=A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(0)}}}L
while by assumption it holds that
(A\cap S)f\cap(A\cap S)g\neq 0
and we are done. So turning to the induction step, suppose that
k+m\geq 1
and that for all non-zero polynomials

f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in AL
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime})+\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g^{\prime})<k+m
, it holds that

Af^{\prime}\cap Ag^{\prime}\neq 0
. Lemma
4.10
grants that
Af
contains an
R
-nice polynomial
\widetilde{f}
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\widetilde{f})=m
. Then considering the leading polynomial-coefficients
p=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(\widetilde{f})
and
q=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(g)
, we get
R
-nice polynomial
in
S
such that

\widetilde{f}-px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}},g-qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\in A
are polynomials with
x
-degree less than
m
while
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(\widetilde{f})
and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(q)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
. In particular, Proposition
4.6
ensures that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(\widetilde{f})=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p)

and
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g)=\mathscr{S}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(q)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q)
.
Next having in view (

⋆

‣
4.3
) above, the technical Lemma
4.11
shows that

upx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=p^{\prime}\lambda
and
u^{\prime}qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=q^{\prime}\lambda
for some elements
u,u^{\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)
and non-zero polynomials
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}L)
.
In particular (since
x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}},\lambda
are regular in
\mathscr{S}
) it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(up)\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p^{\prime})\text{ and }\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(u^{\prime}q)\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q^{\prime});\;\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(u\widetilde{f})\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p^{\prime})\text{ and }\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(u^{\prime}g)\hskip-1.99997pt=\hskip-1.99997pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q^{\prime}).

But since by assumption
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}L
is a uniform left ideal in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
, there are elements

p^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
with

0\neq p^{\prime\prime}p^{\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime},\text{ so that }p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}\neq 0\text{ and }q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g\neq 0\text{ as well}.

The polynomial
h=q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g-p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}
lies in
AL
with

\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(h)<m
. If
h=0
then we are done since in this case,

0\neq q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g=p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}\in Af\cap Ag
. Thus suppose that
h\neq 0
. Then the induction hypothesis yields some
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in A
with

0\neq f^{\prime}f=g^{\prime}h=g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g-g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}\text{ and }g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g=f^{\prime}f+g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}.

Since
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(u\widetilde{f})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p^{\prime})
and

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(u^{\prime}g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q^{\prime})
while
p^{\prime\prime}p^{\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}
,
it follows that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}p^{\prime})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f})
. Thus since also
g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g-g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}\neq 0
, it follows that
g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g\neq 0
,
so that the polynomial
g^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}u^{\prime}g=f^{\prime}f+g^{\prime}p^{\prime\prime}u\widetilde{f}
is a non-zero element in
Af\cap Ag
, completing the proof that
AL
is a uniform left ideal in
A
.

Let
K
be an essential left ideal of
R
that lifts to an essential left ideal in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
for all
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
. Proving that
AK
is essential as left ideal in
A
.
Letting
0\neq f\in A
and
m=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
, we will show by induction on
m
that
AK\cap Af\neq 0
.
In view of the first key Lemma
4.5
, left multiplying
f
by a suitable scalar we may assume that

f
is
R
-nice. If
m=0
, then
f\in A\cap S
and the assumption already grants that

(A\cap S)K\cap(A\cap S)f\neq 0
, so that,

AK\cap Af\neq 0
as well.
Next, suppose that
m\geq 1
and the desired result holds in
x
-degree less than
m
.
In view of (

⋆

‣
4.3
), recall that
the special element
\lambda\in A\cap\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})
,
the associated
R
-subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset A\cap S

and the induced automorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
of

A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
satisfy:
for all
p^{\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
, the polynomial
\lambda p^{\prime}-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(p^{\prime})\lambda\in A
has
x
-degree less than
m
; and for some

u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)
and some non-zero polynomial

p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
it holds that the polynomial
f^{\prime}=uf-p\lambda\in A
has
x
-degree less than
m
.
The assumption on
K
ensures that
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}K
is an essential left ideal in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
;
thus the left ideal
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(K)=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}K)
is essential in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
and it holds for some
v\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
that
0\neq vp\in\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}K)
. Since by definition

\lambda
is a regular element of
\mathscr{S}
it follows that

vp\lambda\neq 0
and the polynomial
vuf=vp\lambda+vf^{\prime}
still has
x
-degree given by
m
.
Notice having in view Lemma
4.5
that, if
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vp\lambda)\nsubset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vuf)
then left multiplying
vuf
by a suitable scalar
c
we may form an
R
-nice polynomial
cvuf\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(cvuf)<m
and then apply the induction hypothesis to produce a non-zero element in
AK\cap Af
.
So we may continue our discussion by assuming that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vp\lambda)\subset\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vuf)
. Then still by Lemma
4.5
, we may further assume that both
vp\lambda
and
vuf
are
R
-nice polynomials with
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vuf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vp\lambda)
. While the non-zero polynomial
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(vp)\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
needs not be
R
-nice, by left multiplying the latter by a suitable scalar from
R
to form an
R
-nice element in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\subset S
and then invoking the second key Lemma
4.10
, we can produce some
R
-nice polynomial
g=(qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}+g^{\prime})\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(vp)
with
q\in S,qx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}},g^{\prime}\in A
such that
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g^{\prime})<m
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g)=m
. But we already know that

wqx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=q^{\prime}\lambda
for some
w\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\cap\mathscr{C}(S)
and some non-zero polynomial
q^{\prime}\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
. Thus,

wg=(q^{\prime}\lambda+wg^{\prime})\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{-1}}}}(vp)=q^{\prime}vp\lambda+\Delta
with
\Delta\in A
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta)<m
;

and letting
p^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{S}}}}(g)
it holds that
q^{\prime}vp\lambda=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{S}}}}(wg)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=wp^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
.

Since
g
is
R
-nice, so is its leading polynomial-coefficient

p^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{S}}}}(g)
and

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p^{\prime})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}})
.
Now since
vuf=vp\lambda+vf^{\prime}
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(vf^{\prime})<m
, the polynomial
q^{\prime}vuf-wg\in A
has
x
-degree less than
m
.
If
q^{\prime}vuf-wg=0
, then
0\neq q^{\prime}vuf=wg\in AK
and we are done.
Next assuming that
q^{\prime}vuf-wg
is non-zero, the induction hypothesis grants some
w^{\prime}\in A
with

0\neq w^{\prime}\cdot(q^{\prime}vuf-wg)\in AK
.

But then, the polynomials
vuf,vp\lambda,g
and
p^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}{S}}}}(g)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
are
R
-nice with

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vuf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(vp\lambda)
,
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}})
and
q^{\prime}vp\lambda=wp^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}
.

Thus Proposition
4.6
shows that

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(vuf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(vp\lambda)
while

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(g)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(p^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}})
, and it follows that:

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q^{\prime}vuf)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(q^{\prime}vp\lambda)=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(wp^{\prime}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}})=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathscr{S}}}}(wg)
.
Whence, the fact that
w^{\prime}\cdot(q^{\prime}vuf-wg)
is non-zero forces
w^{\prime}q^{\prime}vuf
to be non-zero and consequently,
0\neq w^{\prime}q^{\prime}vuf=w^{\prime}\cdot(q^{\prime}vuf-wg)+w^{\prime}wg\in AK
. This completes the proof that
AK
is an essential left ideal in
A
.

For the last claim of the theorem, the additional assumption yields that
every uniform or essential left ideal in
R
lifts to a uniform (or resp., an essential) left ideal in
A
. Next given any left ideals
L,L^{\prime}
in
R
with
L\cap L^{\prime}=0
, let us quickly verify that
AL\cap AL^{\prime}=0
. By our setting for the
R
-ring
S
we know that
SL\cap SL^{\prime}=0
. Hence for the univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective
\mathscr{S}
, Proposition
4.2
grants that
\mathscr{S}L\cap\mathscr{S}L^{\prime}=0
, thus as
R
-subring in
\mathscr{S}
, it holds as well that
AL\cap AL^{\prime}=0
.
Now, Proposition
2.4
applies showing that
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
A
be an essentially special subextension in a univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type
R[x;\alpha,\delta]
, such that
\widetilde{A}a
contains an
R
-nice polynomial of
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}}
for all
0\neq a\in R
.
Then, every uniform or essential left ideal
L
of
R
lifts to a
uniform (or resp., an essential) left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
;

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(x)
;
if
R
has enough uniform left ideals, then so does
A
and
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

Proof.

Except for the claim about the left singular ideal, the other claims of the corollary are granted by Theorem
4.12

specialized to the case that
S
coincides with the ground ring
R
.
It follows in particular that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(AR)
with
AR
considered as left
A
-module. Now the claim that

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}(x)
is the object of a more general result given by Proposition
4.14
below.
∎

Recall by Lemma
4.3
that the reverse lexicographic ordering yields a well-ordering on
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(\boldsymbol{X})
; correspondingly for each
f\in\mathbb{S}
,
we let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{T}}(f)\in\mathbb{T}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)\in R
be respectively the leading term and the leading coefficient of
f
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{S}
be any subring such that
A\cap AL\neq 0
for all non-zero left ideal
L
in
R
. Then for all
f\in\mathbb{S}\raisebox{1.54999pt}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.625]{$\backslash$}}\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
, there exists a non-zero left ideal
L\subset R
with
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
. Consequently,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
, and
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
provided
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(AR)
, with
AR
viewed as left
A
-submodule in
\mathbb{S}
.

Proof.

Let
0\neq f\in\mathbb{S}
,
a=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)
.
We start by assuming that
a\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
. Then it holds for some non-zero left ideal
L
of
R
that
L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)=0
.
We shall reason by transfinite induction on the indexing ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
to show that
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
. Since the base case is obvious, we turn to the induction step and suppose that the desired result holds for all ordinal less than
\mathfrak{n}
. Now
\mathfrak{n}
is either a limit ordinal or a successor ordinal. The case that
\mathfrak{n}
is a limit ordinal is also straightforward. Indeed for this case,
\mathbb{S}=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa<\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is an increasing union of truncated skew polynomial rings of bijective type over
R
while for some fixed
\kappa_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}}
,
f\in\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{0}}}}\leq\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
. But,

\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa_{0}\leq\kappa<\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}}}}(f)
and

\mathbb{S}L=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa_{0}\leq\kappa<\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
, while the induction hypothesis grants that
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}}}}(f)=0
for
\kappa_{0}\leq\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
, which shows that
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
.
Next consider the case of a successor ordinal

\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
. Then setting
x=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}},\alpha=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
and
\delta=\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
, we get that
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x;\alpha,\delta]
is a univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type over the truncated skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
of bijective type over
R
. Letting
m=\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)
, one writes:
f=px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}+f^{\prime}
with
p\in\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
,
f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}
and
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime})<m
. By the definition of the reverse lexicographic ordering,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(p)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)=a
.
So the induction hypothesis grants that
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}}}}(p)=0
. Since the conjugation map
\alpha
is bijective over
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
, the set of univariate terms
\mathbb{T}(x)=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptn\in\mathbb{N}\}
also serves as right
R
-basis for

\mathbb{S}
. So every element
g\in\mathbb{S}L
expresses as

g=x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n_{1}}}}w_{1}+\dotsm+x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n_{k}}}}w_{k}
with

k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}>\dotsm>n_{k}
and
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
. Suppose that
gf=0
. Then
since
gf=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n_{1}}}}}(w_{1}p)x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n_{1}+m}}}+g^{\prime}
for some
g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(g^{\prime})<n_{1}+m
, one must have
w_{1}p=0
, so that,
w_{1}=0
by the transfinite induction hypothesis. Thus inductively, one finds that
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{k}
all vanish and
g=0
. Hence we have proved that
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
under the assumption that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f)\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
.

Now for a general polynomial
f\in\mathbb{S}
that does not live in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
, we may write
f=g+f^{\prime}
with
g\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
and

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f^{\prime})\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
. So let
L^{\prime}
be a non-zero left ideal of
R
with

L^{\prime}\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{lc}}(f^{\prime}))=0
. From above, it holds that
\mathbb{S}L^{\prime}\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f^{\prime})=0
. Since in any ring the intersection of finitely many essential left ideals must be an essential left ideal, we get that
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)
is an essential left ideal of
R
. Hence, setting
L=\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(g)\cap L^{\prime}
yields a non-zero left ideal in
R
with
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
as expected.

Turning to the second part of the proposition, let
A\subset\mathbb{S}
be any subring with
A\cap AL\neq 0
for every non-zero left ideal
L
in
R
. Given any polynomial
f\in A
with
f\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
, the above paragraph grants some non-zero left ideal
L
of
R
with
\mathbb{S}L\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\mathbb{S}}}}(f)=0
, so that,
(A\cap AL)\cap\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(f)=0
while
A\cap AL
is a non-zero left ideal in
A
; hence,
f\notin\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
, which shows that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
. Finally given the additional assumption that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
is contained in the singular ideal of the left
A
-submodule
AR
of
\mathbb{S}
, we have to prove that

A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
. Let

g=a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{1}}}}+\dotsm+a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in A
with
0\neq m\in\mathbb{N}
and
(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}},\tau_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i}}}})\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\times\mathbb{T}
for
1\leq i\leq m
.
Since
a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
lies in
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(AR)
, each
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is an essential left ideal in
A
and so,

\cap_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{i=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(a_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
is still an essential left ideal in
A
that is contained in
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(g)
. Hence,
g\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We now continue with a multivariate version of Theorem
4.12
.

Theorem 4.15
.

Suppose that each derivation map associated with
\mathbb{S}
vanishes on
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
. Let
A
be an essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
. Then, every uniform or essential left ideal
L
in
R
lifts to a uniform (or resp., an essential) left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
;
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
.
And if
R
contains enough uniform left ideals, then so does
A
and
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
.

It’s worth pointing our that, even when one specializes to the classical setting of the ambient skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}
(as special subextension of itself),
the above theorem is still more stronger than its classical analogue
[
Mat95
, theorem 2.4]
in the following aspects:

❍

\mathsf{udim}(R)
needs not be finite and the set
\boldsymbol{X}
of variables needs not be finite;

❍

the assumption that
\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}(\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T})=0
for all
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
is implied by the classical more stronger condition that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)=0
; moreover, we obtain that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
, generalizing the classical fact that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\mathbb{S})=0
if
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)=0
while
\boldsymbol{X}
is finite.

Proof.

Using induction on
\mathfrak{n}
, we start by proving the following first claim.

Every non-zero left ideal
L
of
R
lifts to a
nicely essential submodule
A\cap AL
of the non-zero left
A
-module
AL
.
If
\mathfrak{n}
is the zero ordinal, then
\mathbb{S}
reduces to the ground ring
R
and
A
becomes a nicely essential subring in
R
, so that,
A\cap AL
is obviously a nicely essential left
A
-submodule in
AL
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{n}
is non-zero
while the desired result holds for all ordinal
\kappa<\mathfrak{n}
.
For a limit ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
, (by the definition of an essentially special subextension of
\mathbb{S}
) it holds for some subset
\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{n}
that
A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\Lambda}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is an increasing union of truncated subrings where each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa\in\Lambda
is an essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. The induction hypothesis applies yielding that each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
for
\kappa\in\Lambda
is a nicely essential left
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
-submodule in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
. Since every finite subset in
AL
must entirely live in some
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
with
\kappa\in\Lambda
, it follows that

A\cap AL
is still a nicely essential left
A
-submodule in
AL
. Next turning to the successor ordinal case,
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
for some ordinal
\kappa
. Here
A=A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa+1}}}
is by Definition
4.7
a nicely essential subring of some
R
-subring
\widetilde{A}
. Thus
A
is a nicely essential left
A
-submodule in
\widetilde{A}
, and so is
A\cap AL
in the non-zero left
A
-submodule
AL
of
\widetilde{A}
. This completes the proof of our first claim.

As an immediate consequence,
Proposition
4.2
yields that every direct sum
\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}L_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}}
of non-zero left ideals of the ground ring
R
lifts to a direct sum

\oplus_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i\in I}}}(A\cap AL_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}i}}})
of non-zero left ideals in
A
.
Thus in view of applying Proposition
2.4
(in its full strength), the last part of the theorem follows by the claim about uniform and essential left ideals, which we shall now prove.

Given a uniform left ideal
L\subset R
and an essential left ideal
K\subset R
,
A\cap AL
is uniform while
A\cap AK
is essential as left ideals in
A
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
.

We shall reason by transfinite induction on the ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
.
If
\mathfrak{n}
is the zero ordinal, then
\mathbb{S}
reduces to the ground ring
R
and
A
is a nicely essential subring in
R
, so that, the desired result is granted by Proposition
2.6
.
So turning to the induction step, we suppose that the ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
is non-zero while the desired result holds for every ordinal smaller than
\mathfrak{n}
.

The limit ordinal case
.  As already observed a couple of times before, for the lifting of uniform or essential ideals we shall see that this limit ordinal case essentially reduces to an application of the induction hypothesis; but for the characterisation of the left singular ideals, an indirect trick is necessary. Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(c)}
yields some subset
\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{n}
with
A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\Lambda}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
arising as an increasing union of subrings such that each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa\in\Lambda
is an essentially special subextension of the truncated skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. Thus the induction hypothesis yields for all
\kappa\in\Lambda
that
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
and
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}K
are respectively uniform and essential as left deals in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
.
Given any non-zero polynomials
f,g\in A\cap AL
and
h\in A
, they depend on finitely many variables and it holds for some
\kappa\in\Lambda
that
f,g\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}L
while
h\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
; it follows that

Af\cap Ag\supset A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}f\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}g\neq 0
and
(A\cap AK)\cap Ah\supset(A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}K)\cap A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}h\neq 0
.
In particular, for every
a\in\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
,
A\cap A\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(a)
is an essential left ideal of
A
contained in
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A}}}(a)
, showing that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(AR)
. Since moreover every non-zero left ideal
I
of
R
lifts to a non-zero left ideal
A\cap AI
in
A
, Proposition
4.14
applies showing that

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
.
So the desired result is proved in the limit ordinal case.

The successor ordinal case:
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
.
As we shall now see, this very involved step has actually been handled by Theorem
4.12
. Throughout, we set:

x=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
,

\alpha=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
,
\delta=\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
and
S=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}

so that,
\mathbb{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
.

In view of applying main Theorem
4.12
,
the ring
S=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is a multivariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type over the coefficient ring
R
,
having the following properties, where
\mathsf{(i,ii)}
are expected by the setting of
Theorem
4.12
.

\mathsf{(i)}

S
is already free as left
R
-module with
R
-basis given by the truncated subset of standard terms
\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}=\mathbb{T}(x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\eta}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\eta\in\kappa)
; and by virtue of Proposition
4.6
,
every
R
-nice polynomial
p\in S
enjoys a nice annihilator in
S
:
\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}S}}}(p)=S\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}R}}}(p)
.

\mathsf{(ii)}

Every direct sum of left ideals in
R
lifts to a direct sum of left ideals in
S
, (Proposition
4.2
).

\mathsf{(iii)}

By the transfinite induction hypothesis,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S)=\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. By the main assumption of the theorem,
\delta(\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(S))=\delta(\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}})=0
.

Now, by Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(c)}
we know that
A
is a nicely essential subring of some prescribed special subextension
\widetilde{A}
in
\mathbb{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
. Since the induced subring
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}})}}}\subset S
is at least an
R
-subring, Proposition
4.14
grants that

\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}})}}})\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
, so that,

R\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}})}}})\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)
. This fact together with property
\mathsf{(iii)}
above show that
Lemma
4.10
\mathsf{(c)}
applies, yielding the crucial fact that:

\widetilde{A}
already satisfies the main condition of Theorem
4.12
about
R
-nice polynomial of prescribed degree:
\widetilde{A}a
contains an
R
-nice polynomial of
x
-degree
\mathsf{d}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\widetilde{A}}}}
for all non-zero scalar
a\in R
.

Moreover and still by Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(c)}
, for each attainable
x
-degree
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\widetilde{A})
the induced subring
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
is still an essentially special subextension of the truncated skew polynomial ring
S=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
.
Thus the induction hypothesis guaranties that
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}L
is uniform while
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}K
is essential as left ideals in
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
.
We have therefore shown that, for the essentially special subextension
A
of the univariate skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
, all the hypotheses of Theorem
4.12
are satisfied, granting that the left ideals
A\cap AL
and
A\cap AK
are respectively uniform and essential in
A
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\subset\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(AR)
while it is already granted by the first claim of the present proof that
A\cap AI\neq 0
for all non-zero left ideal
I
of
R
. Hence, Proposition
4.14
applies yielding that
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
.
This completes the proof that every uniform or essential left ideal
L
in
R
lifts to a uniform (or resp., an essential) left ideal
A\cap AL
in
A
and
\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(A)=A\cap\boldsymbol{\zetaup}(R)\mathbb{T}
. Hence, the proof of the theorem is complete.
∎

We shall now investigate the (semi)primeness of essentially special subextensions in
\mathbb{S}
; as usual,
‘(semi)prime’ is a short form for ‘prime (or respectively, semiprime)’.
Allowing an ambient univariate skew polynomial ring

\mathscr{S}=S[x;\alpha,\delta]
of injective type, for an
R
-subring
A\subset\mathscr{S}
we say that:

A
is a
special subextension of injective type
in
\mathscr{S}

provided
A
has the special shape of Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(a)}
in which, for
0\neq m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
,
the induced endomorphism
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}
of the associated
R
-subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
is only required to be injective.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\mathscr{S}=S[x;\sigma,\delta]
be a univariate skew polynomial of injective type, and
A\subset\mathscr{S}
any special subextension of injective type.
Assume that
A\cap S
has the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators. Then
A
is (semi)prime provided so is each induced subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
for every
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
.

Proof.

It helps recording by
[
LM03
, Proposition 3.6]
that: if
S
is (semi)prime, then so is
\mathscr{S}
. The arguments proving this result shall be adapted and generalized to the present setting. Let
I
be an ideal in
A
and
m\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
; for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define:

I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}=\{p\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0pt\exists w\in\mathscr{S},\,p\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}+w\in I,\,\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(w)<nm\}.

(\text{{\sc{i}}})
.

If
I
contains a non-zero polynomial of degree
m
, then
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,1}}}\neq 0
and
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,1}}}\neq 0
for every
d\in\mathrm{Deg}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(A)
.
Indeed, suppose that
I
contains a non-zero polynomial
f
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f)=m
. We have
f=px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}+f^{\prime}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\neq p\in S
and
f^{\prime}\in A
with
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(f^{\prime})<m
. The special shape of
A
grants some
u\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
regular as element of
S
with

upx^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}=p^{\prime}\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\neq 0
, which shows that
0\neq p^{\prime}\in I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,1}}}
. Next let
d
be any attainable
x
-degree in
A
, thus we are granted with the special element
\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}d}}}\in A\cap\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})
with

\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}\in\mathscr{C}(S)\subset\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})
.
We get that

\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}uf=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}d}}}p^{\prime}\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}+\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}uf^{\prime}=\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}(p^{\prime}\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}})x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m+d}}}+\Delta\in I,

where the element
\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}(p^{\prime}\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}})\in S
is non-zero (because so is
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}(p^{\prime}\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}})
while
\upsilonup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{d}}}}
is a regular element of
S
) and
\Delta
is some polynomial belonging to
A
and satisfying
\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\Delta)<m+d
. This shows that
I
contains a polynomial of
x
-degree
m+d
and so, as before,
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,1}}}\neq 0
.

(\text{{\sc{ii}}})
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n\in\mathbb{N}}}}
is an increasing sequence of left ideals in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
; for some
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
we have
\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}})
for all
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
; and for all
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k}}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})\subset I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n+k}}}
.  Clearly, each
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}
is a left ideal in
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
; and right multiplying elements of
I
by the special element
\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\in A\cap\mathscr{C}(\mathscr{S})
shows that
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}\subset I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n+1}}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus since the ring
A\cap S
satisfies the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators,
it satisfies the
descending chain condition
on right annihilators
and it holds for some
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\in\mathbb{N}
that
\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}})
for all
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
.
Next, the special shape of
A
guaranties for all
v\in A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
that:

\displaystyle\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}v-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}(v)\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}})<m,\text{ which implies for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$ that }

\displaystyle\deg_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}x}}}(\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}v-\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(v)\lambdaup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}})<nm.

It follows for all
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
that
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{k}}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})\subset I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n+k}}}
.

(\text{{\sc{iii}}})
.

Now proving that
A
is (semi)prime if so is each induced subring
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
for every attainable
x
-degree
m
.
With sections
(\text{{\sc{i,ii}}})
of the present proof, the following remaining arguments
(essentially) repeat those proving the ordinary
univariate analogue of
[
LM03
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Let
I,J
be any ideals of
A
with
IJ=0
.

Given each attainable
x
-degree
m
in
A
, we first check that the natural number
N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
(afforded by section
(\text{{\sc{ii}}})
) satisfies:
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}=0
for all
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
.
Indeed letting
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}
, it holds that

\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}})
. The relation
IJ=0
yields in particular that
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=0
, so that,
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})\subset\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=\mathsf{r}\hskip-1.99997pt.\hskip-1.00006pt\mathPzc{a}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}A\cap S}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}})
and
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}}\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=0
as well. But
\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})\subset I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,2n}}}

and we get that:

\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{(m)}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{n}}}}(J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}})=0
, so that,

I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,n}}}=0
as expected.

Now given the assumption that each subring

A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
is prime for every attainable
x
-degree
m
in
A
, and further assuming that
J\neq 0
, we have to check that
I=0
. Let
m
be any fixed attainable
x
-degree. Since
J\neq 0
, section
(\text{{\sc{i}}})
of the proof shows that
J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}d,1}}}\neq 0
for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
, and since
m
is already an attainable
x
-degree in
A
, we equally have still by
(\text{{\sc{i}}})
that
J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,1}}}\neq 0
, so that
J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,n}}}\neq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
(because the sequence
(J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,n}}})_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}n}}}
is increasing). But then,
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,n}}}J_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,n}}}=0
for all
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d}}}
and the primeness of
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m+d)}}}
grants that
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,n}}}=0
for all
n\geq N_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d}}}
.
In particular,
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+d,1}}}=0
and still by
(\text{{\sc{i}}})
it follows that
I_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,1}}}=0
. The latter being true for all attainable
x
-degree
m
, another application of section
(\text{{\sc{i}}})
yields that
I=0
as expected, so that the ring
A
is prime. Likewise, starting with the assumption that each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
is semiprime,
the previous discussion with
J=I
shows that

A
is semiprime as well.
∎

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
R
be a (semi)prime left Goldie ring and
A
any essentially special subextension of
\mathbb{S}
.
Then
A
is left Goldie with
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(R)
, and
A
is also (semi)prime provided it is a strongly essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
.

Before writing the proof, one observes that for every
R
-subring
B
of
\mathbb{S}
generated as left
R
-module by a subset of
\mathbb{T}
, if
B
is left Goldie then so is
R
because
\mathsf{udim}(R)\leq\mathsf{udim}(B)
by virtue of
Proposition
4.2
.

Proof.

The coefficient ring
R
being by assumption (semi)prime left Goldie, Goldie’s theorem yields that
R
is also left non-singular. It follows by Proposition
4.14
that both
\mathbb{S}
and
A
are left non-singular, as well as every subring
B\subset\mathbb{S}
with
B\cap BL\neq 0
for all non-zero left ideal
L
in
R
. Since moreover as a ring with finite uniform dimension,
R
has enough uniform left ideals, Theorem
4.15
yields that
\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})=\mathsf{udim}(R)=\mathsf{udim}(A)
.
Hence, to conclude in particular that the ambient skew polynomial ring

\mathbb{S}
is (semi)prime left Goldie, it only remains by Goldie’s theorem to prove that
\mathbb{S}
is (semi)prime.

With the facts already granted by the previous paragraph,
we shall continue proving the theorem using transfinite induction on the indexing ordinal
\mathfrak{n}
.
When
\mathfrak{n}
is the zero ordinal,
\mathbb{S}
coincides with the (semi)prime left Goldie coefficient ring
R
while
A
is a nicely essential subring in
R
or a strongly nicely essential subring in
R
in case the additional assumption on
A
is taken into account. Hence thanks to Proposition
2.7
, all the claims of the theorem hold for the case of the zero ordinal.

We then turn to the induction step with
\mathfrak{n}
non-zero and assume that the theorem holds for every ordinal less than
\mathfrak{n}
. Starting with the limit ordinal case,
the ambient skew polynomial ring

\mathbb{S}=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}}}}\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is evidently (semi)prime as increasing union of rings each of which is (semi)prime by the induction hypothesis. Thus together with the introductory paragraph, we get that
\mathbb{S}
is a (semi)prime left Goldie ring and, since moreover the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators is transferred to subrings,
A
is left Goldie, as well as each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
for
\kappa\in\mathfrak{n}
such that
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is an essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. Next additionally assume that
A
is a strongly essentially special subextension of
\mathbb{S}
. Then Definition
4.7
\mathsf{(c)}
yields some subset
\Lambda\subset\mathfrak{n}
such that
A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\Lambda}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
and for every
\kappa\in\Lambda
, each
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is still a strongly essentially special subextension of the truncated skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. Thus the induction hypothesis grants that
A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is (semi)prime for
\kappa\in\Lambda
, and so the ring
A=\cup_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa\in\Lambda}}}A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
is (semi)prime as union of (semi)prime rings. This completes the proof of the theorem for the limit ordinal case.

We now continue with the successor ordinal case:
\mathfrak{n}=\kappa+1=\kappa\cup\{\kappa\}
for some ordinal
\kappa
. As usual, set
x=x_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}},\alpha=\alpha_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
and
\delta=\delta_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{\kappa}}}}
, so that,

\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x;\alpha,\delta]
is a univariate skew polynomial ring of bijective type
over
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. For all
ordinal
\kappa^{\prime}\leq\kappa
, the induction hypothesis grants that each
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa^{\prime}}}}
is (semi)prime and hence (semi)prime left Goldie as a (semi)prime left non-singular ring with finite uniform dimension. In particular,
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
already has the
ascending chain condition
on left annihilators, and Proposition
4.16
applies showing that
\mathbb{S}
is (semi)prime. Thus,
\mathbb{S}
is semiprime left Goldie, and its subring
A
(which already has finite uniform dimension) is left Goldie. It remains to see that
A
is (semi)prime under the additional assumption that
A
is a strongly essentially special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
. By definition the ring
A=A_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa+1}}}
is a strongly nicely essential subring in some special subextension
\widetilde{A}
of the univariate skew polynomial
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}[x;\alpha,\delta]
, and for every attainable
x
-degree
m
in
\widetilde{A}
, it is required that the associated subring
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
be also a strongly essentially special subextension of the truncated skew polynomial ring
\mathbb{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\kappa}}}
. So the induction hypothesis grants that each
\widetilde{A}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}(m)}}}
is (semi)prime and invoking again Proposition
4.16
yields that
\widetilde{A}
is (semi)prime. But then, thanks to Proposition
2.6
\mathsf{(a)}
,
\mathsf{udim}(\widetilde{A})=\mathsf{udim}(A)
is already finite while
\widetilde{A}
is already left non-singular (as granted by the the introductory paragraph of the present proof).
So we have reached the conclusion that
\widetilde{A}
is a (semi)prime left Goldie ring, and it follows by virtue of Proposition
2.7
that
A
is (semi)prime, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.
Some essentially special subextensions in a two-variate skew polynomial ring

We consider integer parameters

p,e,r\geq 2\text{ and }c,d\geq 1,\text{ with
$p$ a prime number and $d$ a divisor of $e-1$}.

The ambient ring is a two-variate Ore extension
\mathbb{S}=R\cramped{\langle x,y:yx=(x+px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}})\rangle}
with natural left
R
-basis given by the set

\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(x,y)=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptm,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
of standard terms in the independent variables
x,y
.
Write
\mathsf{term}(f)
for the set of all terms appearing in each
f\in\mathbb{S}
.
Roughly our setting will enable us to investigate for each pair
a,b
of positive integers
the uniform dimension of the subextension
A
of
\mathbb{S}
generated over
R
by the term
x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}a}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}b}}}
and satisfying the condition that
\mathsf{term}(f)\subset A
for every non-zero polynomial
f
in
A
.

Recall for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
that, the binomial coefficient
\binom{n}{m}=\dfrac{n!}{m!(n-m)!}
vanishes when
0\leq n<m
. We need the following computation of the
p
-adic valuation of
\binom{p^{r}}{n}p^{n}
for
1\leq n\leq p^{r}
, where
\mathpzc{v}_{p}(n)
is the greatest natural number
k
with
p^{k}\mid n
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For each
n\in\llbracket 1\hskip 1.00006pt,\hskip 1.00006ptp^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}r}}}\rrbracket
, write
\mathpzc{s}_{p}(n)
for the sum of the base-
p
digits of
n
. We have
\mathpzc{v}_{p}\bigl(\binom{p^{r}}{n}p^{n}\bigr)\geq r+n-\mathpzc{v}_{p}(n!)\geq r+\frac{(p-2)n+\mathpzc{s}_{p}(n)}{p-1}\geq r+1
.

Proof.

Since
p
is a prime number,
\mathpzc{v}_{p}(uv)=\mathpzc{v}_{p}(u)\mathpzc{v}_{p}(v)
for all
0\neq u,v\in\mathbb{N}
; and it is clear that

\mathpzc{v}_{p}\bigl(\binom{p^{r}}{n}p^{n}\bigr)\geq r+n-\mathpzc{v}_{p}(n!)
.
But then, letting
{\displaystyle{\ell}}
be the number of base-
p
digits of
n
,
the Legendre formula gives

\mathpzc{v}_{p}(n!)=\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s=1}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{{\displaystyle{\ell}}}}}}\lfloor n/p^{s}\rfloor=\frac{n-\mathpzc{s}_{p}(n)}{p-1}\leq\frac{n-1}{p-1}
, which yields the desired result.
∎

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
R
has characteristic
p^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}r+1}}}
. Then we get an
R
-ring automorphism
\alpha:R[x]\mspace{1.8mu}\hbox to31pt{\vbox to5.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt\lower-2.44pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{}{}{}}}{{
{}{}{}}}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\mspace{1.8mu}R[x]
with
\alpha(x)=x+px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}}
, having finite order
p^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}r}}}
.
Thus the terms
x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}p^{r}}}}
and
y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}p^{r}}}}
are central elements
in the Ore extension
\mathbb{S}=R[x][y;\alpha]
.

Proof.

For
0\neq g\in R[x]
, the valuation
\mathpzc{v}_{x}(g)
denotes the largest
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
x^{k}
dividing
g
; while
\mathpzc{v}_{x}(0)=\infty
.
The definition of
\alpha
readily yields for every
f\in R[x]
that
\alpha(f)=f+pg
for some
g\in R[x]
with
\mathpzc{v}_{x}(g)>\mathpzc{v}_{x}(f-f(0))
.
Thus one can inductively form
elements
u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}},u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}2}}},\ldots
in
R[x]
with:

\displaystyle u_{0}=x,u_{1}=x^{e},\;\alpha(u_{m})=u_{m}+pu_{m+1},\,\mathpzc{v}_{x}(u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+1}}})>\mathpzc{v}_{x}(u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}})\text{ and }

\displaystyle\alpha^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}(x)=\hskip-3.00003pt\tsum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k=0}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m}}}}\textstyle{\binom{m}{k}}p^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}k}}}u_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}k}}}\text{ for all }m\in\mathbb{N}.

Thanks to Lemma
5.1
, the natural number
\binom{p^{r}}{k}p^{k}
is a multiple of the characteristic
p^{r+1}
of
R
for every
k\geq 1
. And it follows from the above relation that
p^{r}
is the least positive integer
m
with
\alpha^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}(x)=x
, proving that
\alpha
is an
R
-ring automorphism with finite order
p^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}r}}}
.
As an immediate consequence,

\alpha(x^{p^{r}})=(x+px^{e})^{p^{r}}=x^{p^{r}}\sum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{k=0}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{p^{r}}}}}\binom{p^{r}}{k}p^{k}x^{k(e-1)}=x^{p^{r}}
. Thus
x^{p^{r}},y^{p^{r}}
live in the center of
\mathbb{S}
.
∎

Next for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the following bounds
\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}=\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}(e),\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}=\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}r,c}}}(e)\in\mathbb{N}
and
\overline{\mathrm{S}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}=\overline{\mathrm{S}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m,r,c}}}(e)
:

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}

\displaystyle=\tsum_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\scriptstyle{s=0}}}}^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}\scriptstyle{m-1}}}}e^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}s}}}=\frac{e^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}-1}{e-1};\;\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}\text{ satisfies the relation: }c\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}-1}}}\leq r<c\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}}}},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{S}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}

\displaystyle=c\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\text{ for }0\leq m\leq\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}};\;\overline{\mathrm{S}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}+s}}}=c(s+\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}}}})+sr(e-1)\text{ for }s\in\mathbb{N}.

It is clear that:

\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}0}}}=0,\,\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}1}}}=1\leq r,\,\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m+1}}}=1+\mathrm{S}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}e
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

We then consider the two following parametrized
sets of terms (depending on the integer parameters
c,d\geq 1
set above wit
d\mid e-1
):

(5.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptm,n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }n-cm\in d\mathbb{Z}\};

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}=\{x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}n}}}y^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}m}}}\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptm,n\in\mathbb{N},\,n-cm\in d\mathbb{N},\,n\leq\overline{\mathrm{S}}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}m}}}\}.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
R
has characteristic
p^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}r+1}}}
.
Then the left
R
-submodules
A=R\mathsf{T}
and
\widetilde{A}=R\widetilde{\mathsf{T}}
of the Ore extension
\mathbb{S}=R\cramped{\langle x,y\hskip 3.0pt\colon\hskip 3.0ptyx=(x+px^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}e}}})y\rangle}

are subrings such that
A
is a strongly nicely essential subring in
\widetilde{A}
while the latter is is a special subextension in
\mathbb{S}
.

Before we turn to the deeply involved proof of the proposition, the reader is prompted on the following points, some of which follow from the details of the proof below.

\mathsf{(i)}

If
d=e-1
, then
A
is the subextension of
\mathbb{S}
characterized by:
x^{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.5mu}c}}}y\in A
and
\mathsf{term}(f)\subset A
for all
f\in A
.

\mathsf{(ii)}

The ring
A
is not a skew monoid ring over
R
and it may not be viewed as skew polynomial ring extension of
R
in any useful way. The same is true for
\widetilde{A}
, unless
d
equals
1
where in the last case
\widetilde{A}
coincides with
\mathbb{S}
.

\mathsf{(iii)}

Assume the case that
d\neq 1
. Then the essentially special subextension
A
of
\mathbb{S}
is not a special subextension and one may not rely on classical techniques to directly investigate the uniform dimension of
A
. The special subextension
\widetilde{A}
of
\mathbb{S}
is not an essential subring of
\mathbb{S}
.

\mathsf{(iv)}

Consequently,

\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\widetilde{A})
.
If
R
has enough uniform left ideals, then
Theorem
4.12
(or better, Theorem
4.15
) applies yielding that:
the same holds for
A
and
\widetilde{A}
and
\mathsf{udim}(A)=\mathsf{udim}(\widetilde{A})=\mathsf{udim}(R)=\mathsf{udim}(\mathbb{S})
.
If
R
is (semi)prime left Goldie then the same holds for both
A
and
\widetilde{A}
thanks to Theorem
4.17
.

m,n\in\mathbb{N}

.

.\begin{ItemList}\itemize@item@[\pmsf(i).]{Case that $m+m^{\prime}\leq\boldsymbol{m}_{{\cramped[\scriptstyle]{\mspace{-0.72mu}}}}$}. Here,
